Any domain-wall solution interpolating between two BTZ geometries, with no other non-trivial asymptotic backgrounds should be likewise dual to a NESS of a perfectly-scrambling interface. We suspect that many top-down solutions of this kind exist, but they are hard to find. Indeed, although many BPS domain walls are known in the supergravity literature, their finite-temperature counterparts are rare. The one example that we are aware of is the Janus AdS
3
black brane
[
164
]
. But even for this computationally-friendly example, the far-from-equilibrium stationary solutions are not known.

3.3
Stationary branes

To simplify the problem we will here resort to the more tractable thin-brane approximation, hoping that it captures some of the essential physics of the stationary states. This model is the one described in sec.
1.7.2
, as well as the one studied in the related papers
[
24
,
77
,
189
]
.

3.3.1
General setup

Consider two BTZ metrics (
3.1
) glued along a thin brane whose worldvolume is parametrized by
\tau
and
\sigma
. Much like in the previous chapter, its embedding in the two coordinate patches (
j=1,2
) is given by six functions
\{r_{j}(\tau,\sigma),\,t_{j}(\tau,\sigma),\,x_{j}(\tau,\sigma)\}
. Here, we must allow a little bit more generality than in the static case due to the loss of the time-inversion symmetry in the stationary case. The most general ansatz, such
that the induced metric is
\tau
-independent, is of the form :

x_{j}=x_{j}(\sigma),\ \ r_{j}=r_{j}(\sigma),\ \ t_{j}=\tau+f_{j}(\sigma)\ .

(3.22)

We will denote by
h_{ab}
the metric induced on the worldsheet of the membrane.

Compared to the static case, in (
3.22
) we allow for the addition of the function
f_{j}(\sigma)
to the time coordinate of the membrane. We will see that extension of the ansatz will prove to be necessary to find solutions. In principle, one can multiply
\tau
on the right-hand side by constants
a_{j}^{-1}
. But the metric (
3.1
) is invariant under rescaling of the coordinates
r\to ar
,
(t,x)\to a^{-1}(t,x)
, and of the parameters
(M,J)\to a^{2}(M,J)
, so we may absorb the
a_{j}
into a redefinition of the parameters
M_{j},J_{j}
. Hence, without loss of generality, we set
a_{j}=1
.

Following the same convention as in chap.
2
, we choose the parameter
\sigma
to be the redshift factor squared
6
6

6

This is a slight misnomer, since
\sigma
becomes negative in the ergoregion. Nonetheless, we will adopt this jargon introduced in chap.
2
for a stationary observer

\displaystyle\sigma=r_{1}^{2}-M_{1}\ell_{1}^{2}=r_{2}^{2}-M_{2}\ell_{2}^{2}\ .

(3.23)

Eq. (
3.23
) is one of the metric matching equations (
2.4
). Of the remaining embedding functions, the sum
f_{1}+f_{2}
is pure gauge (it can be absorbed by a reparametrization of
\tau
) whereas the time delay across the wall,
\Delta t(\sigma)\equiv f_{2}(\sigma)-f_{1}(\sigma)
, is a physical quantity. This and the two functions
x_{j}(\sigma)
should be determined by solving the three remaining
equations: (i) The rest of the metric matching conditions equating
h_{\tau\sigma}
and
h_{\sigma\sigma}
, and (ii) one of the (trace-reversed) Israel-Lanczos conditions (
2.18
), which we remind here :

K^{1}_{ab}+K^{2}_{ab}=\lambda h_{ab}\ .

(
2.18
)

The conventions used are the same as in the previous chapter, namely the normal vector to the wall is outward pointing and
\lambda
is the brane tension.

3.3.2
Solution of the equations

In this section we solve the Israel-Lanczos equations. According to the convention chosen above, the solution is given in the ‘folded setup’ where the interface is a conformal boundary for the product theory CFT
{}_{1}\otimes\,
CFT
2
. Unfolding side
j
amounts to sending
x_{j}\to-x_{j}
and
J_{j}\to-J_{j}
.

3.4
Solving the thin-brane equations

From the form (
3.1
) of the bulk metric and the embedding ansatz (
3.22
) of a stationary brane, we derive the following continuity equations for the induced metric. In this section we write
\hat{g}_{ab}
for the induced metric instead of
h_{ab}
, to prevent any confusion with the function
h(r)
:

\displaystyle\hat{g}_{\tau\tau}

\displaystyle=M_{1}\ell_{1}^{2}-r_{1}^{2}=M_{2}\ell_{2}^{2}-r_{2}^{2}\ ,

(3.24)

\displaystyle\hat{g}_{\tau\sigma}

\displaystyle=(M_{1}\ell_{1}^{2}-r_{1}^{2})f_{1}^{\prime}-\frac{J_{1}\ell_{1}}{2}x_{1}^{\prime}=(M_{2}\ell_{2}^{2}-r_{2}^{2})f_{2}^{\prime}-\frac{J_{2}\ell_{2}}{2}x_{2}^{\prime}\ ,

(3.25)

\displaystyle\hat{g}_{\sigma\sigma}

\displaystyle=\frac{\ell_{1}^{2}r_{1}^{\prime 2}}{h_{1}(r_{1})}+r_{1}^{2}x_{1}^{\prime 2}-J_{1}\ell_{1}x_{1}^{\prime}f_{1}^{\prime}+(M_{1}\ell_{1}^{2}-r_{1}^{2})f_{1}^{\prime 2}\ ,

\displaystyle=\frac{\ell_{2}^{2}r_{2}^{\prime 2}}{h_{2}(r_{2})}+r_{2}^{2}x_{2}^{\prime 2}-J_{2}\ell_{2}x_{2}^{\prime}f_{2}^{\prime}+(M_{2}\ell_{2}^{2}-r_{2}^{2})f_{2}^{\prime 2}\ .

(3.26)

The primes denote derivatives with respect to
\sigma
, and the function
h(r)
has been defined in (
3.2
),

h(r)={r^{2}}-M\ell^{2}+\frac{J^{2}\ell^{2}}{4r^{2}}=\frac{1}{r^{2}}(r^{2}-r_{+}^{2})(r^{2}-r_{-}^{2})\ .

(
3.2
)

Following sec.
2.3
we choose the convenient parametrization
\sigma=-\hat{g}_{\tau\tau}
, so that
r_{j}^{2}=\sigma+M_{j}\ell_{j}^{2}
and
r_{j}^{\prime}=1/2r_{j}
. This parametrization need not be one-to-one, it will actually only cover half of the wall when this latter has a turning point. With this choice the ergoplane is located at
r_{j}^{2}=M_{j}\ell_{j}^{2}\Longrightarrow\sigma=0
, and the functions
h_{j}
can be written as

h_{j}(\sigma)=\frac{\sigma^{2}+\sigma M_{j}\ell_{j}^{2}+J_{j}^{2}\ell_{j}^{2}/4}{\sigma+M_{j}\ell_{j}^{2}}=\frac{(\sigma-\sigma_{+}^{{\rm H}j})(\sigma-\sigma_{-}^{{\rm H}j})}{\sigma+M_{j}\ell_{j}^{2}}\ ,

(3.27)

where

\displaystyle\sigma_{\pm}^{{\rm H}j}=-\frac{M_{j}\ell_{j}^{2}}{2}\pm\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{M_{j}^{2}\ell_{j}^{4}-J_{j}^{2}\ell_{j}^{2}}

(3.28)

are the locations of the horizons in the
j
th chart.

From (
3.24
)-(
3.26
) one computes the determinant of the induced metric

-{\rm det}(\hat{g})=\frac{\sigma\ell_{j}^{2}}{4r_{j}^{2}h_{j}}+{h_{j}r_{j}^{2}}x_{j}^{\prime\,2}\ .

(3.29)

Note that it does not depend on the time-delay functions
f_{j}(\sigma)
, because these can be absorbed by the unit-Jacobian reparametrization

\displaystyle\tilde{\tau}=\tau+f_{j}(\sigma),\hskip 10.00002pt\tilde{\sigma}=\sigma\ .

(3.30)

Eq.(
3.29
) can be used to express the
x_{j}^{\prime}
(up to a sign) in terms of det
\hat{g}
. A combination of (
3.24
) and (
3.25
) expresses, in turn, the time delay across the wall in terms of the
x_{j}^{\prime}
,

\displaystyle\sigma(f_{2}^{\prime}-f_{1}^{\prime})=\frac{1}{2}(J_{1}\ell_{1}x_{1}^{\prime}-J_{2}\ell_{2}x_{2}^{\prime})\ .

(3.31)

To complete the calculation we need therefore to find det
\hat{g}
and then solve the equations (
3.29
) for
x_{j}^{\prime}
.

This is done with the help of the Israel-Lanczos conditions
[
190
,
135
]
(see sec.
2.2
for more details) which express the discontinuity of the extrinsic curvature across the wall, (
2.18
).

After Mathematica-aided computations, we arrive at the expressions for the extrinsic curvature (see app.
C.1
) :

K_{\tau\tau}=\frac{hr^{2}x^{\prime}}{\ell\sqrt{|\hat{g}|}}\hskip 20.00003pt{\rm and}\hskip 20.00003ptK_{\tau\sigma}=\frac{hr^{2}x^{\prime}}{\sigma\ell\sqrt{|\hat{g}|}}\hat{g}_{\tau\sigma}+\frac{J\sqrt{|\hat{g}|}}{2\sigma}\ ,

(3.32)

where
\hat{h}
is a shorthand notation for
{\rm det}(\hat{g})
. The Israel-Lanczos equations (
2.18
) thus read

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{|\hat{g}}|}\Bigl(\frac{h_{1}r_{1}^{2}x_{1}^{\prime}}{\ell_{1}}+\frac{h_{2}r_{2}^{2}x_{2}^{\prime}}{\ell_{2}}\Bigr)=-\lambda\sigma\ ,

(3.33)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{|\hat{g}}|}\Bigl(\frac{h_{1}r_{1}^{2}x_{1}^{\prime}}{\ell_{1}}+\frac{h_{2}r_{2}^{2}x_{2}^{\prime}}{\ell_{2}}\Bigr)\hat{g}_{\tau\sigma}+\frac{\sqrt{|\hat{g}}|}{2}(J_{1}+J_{2})=-\lambda\sigma\,\hat{g}_{\tau\sigma}\ .

(3.34)

These are compatible if and only if

\displaystyle J_{1}+J_{2}=0\ ,

(3.35)

which translates to energy conservation in the boundary CFT. We have checked that the third equation,
[K_{\sigma\sigma}]=-\lambda\hat{g}_{\sigma\sigma}
, is automatically obeyed and thus redundant.

3.4.1
The general solution

Squaring twice (
3.33
) and using (
3.29
) to eliminate the
x_{j}^{\prime\,2}
leads to a quadratic equation for the determinant. This has a singular solution
{\rm det}(\hat{g})=0
, and a non-pathological one

-{\rm det}(\hat{g})=\lambda^{2}\sigma^{3}\bigg[\frac{4h_{1}h_{2}r_{1}^{2}r_{2}^{2}}{\ell_{1}^{2}\ell_{2}^{2}}-\big(\frac{h_{1}r_{1}^{2}}{\ell_{1}^{2}}+\frac{h_{2}r_{2}^{2}}{\ell_{2}^{2}}-\lambda^{2}\sigma^{2}\big)^{2}\bigg]^{-1}\ .

(3.36)

Inserting the expressions for
r_{j}(\sigma)
and
h_{j}(\sigma)
leads after some algebra to

-{\rm det}(\hat{g})=\frac{\lambda^{2}\sigma}{A\sigma^{2}+2B\sigma+C}\ ,

(3.37)

with coefficients :

\displaystyle A=(\lambda_{\max}^{2}-\lambda^{2})(\lambda^{2}-\lambda_{\min}^{2})\ ,

(3.38)

\displaystyle B=\lambda^{2}(M_{1}+M_{2})-\lambda_{0}^{2}(M_{1}-M_{2})\ ,

\displaystyle C=-(M_{1}-M_{2})^{2}+\lambda^{2}J_{1}^{2}\ .

The critical tensions in these expressions are

\displaystyle\lambda_{\rm min}=\big|\frac{1}{\ell_{1}}-\frac{1}{\ell_{2}}\big|\ ,\hskip 10.00002pt\lambda_{\rm max}=\frac{1}{\ell_{1}}+\frac{1}{\ell_{2}}\ ,\hskip 10.00002pt\lambda_{0}=\sqrt{\lambda_{\rm max}\lambda_{\rm min}}\ .

(3.39)

For a static wall,
i.e.
when
J_{1}=J_{2}=0
, the above formulae reduce, as they should, to the ones obtained ref.
[
189
]
.
7
7

7

When comparing with this reference beware that it uses the (slightly confusing) notation
\hat{g}_{\sigma\sigma}\equiv g(\sigma)
so that, since the metric is diagonal in the static case, det
\hat{g}=-\sigma g(\sigma)
.
The only effect of the non-zero
J_{j}
is actually to shift the coefficient
C
in (
3.38
).

The roots of the quadratic polynomial in the denominator of (
3.37
),

\displaystyle\sigma_{\pm}=\frac{-B\pm\sqrt{B^{2}-AC}}{A}\ ,

(3.40)

determine the behaviour of the solution. If
\sigma_{+}
is either complex or negative (part of) the brane worldvolume has
{\rm det}\,\hat{g}>0
in the ergoregion, so it is spacelike and physically unacceptable. Acceptable solutions have
\sigma_{+}>0
or
\sigma_{+}=0
, and describe walls that avoid, respectively enter the ergoregion as explained in the main text, see section
3.5
.

The actual shape of the wall is found by inserting (
3.37
) in (
3.29
) and solving for
x_{j}^{\prime\,2}
. After some rearrangements this gives

\displaystyle\epsilon_{1}\frac{x_{1}^{\prime}}{\ell_{1}}

\displaystyle=\frac{(\lambda^{2}+\lambda_{0}^{2})\sigma+(M_{1}-M_{2})}{2(\sigma+M_{1}\ell_{1}^{2}+J^{2}\ell_{1}^{2}/4\sigma)\sqrt{A\sigma(\sigma-\sigma_{+})(\sigma-\sigma_{-})}}\ ,

(3.41)

\displaystyle\epsilon_{2}\frac{x_{2}^{\prime}}{\ell_{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{(\lambda^{2}-\lambda_{0}^{2})\sigma-(M_{1}-M_{2})}{2(\sigma+M_{2}\ell_{2}^{2}+J^{2}\ell_{2}^{2}/4\sigma)\sqrt{A\sigma(\sigma-\sigma_{+})(\sigma-\sigma_{-})}}\ ,

(3.42)

where
\epsilon_{j}=\pm
are signs. They are fixed by the linear equation (
3.33
) with the result

\displaystyle\epsilon_{j}(\sigma)=-\frac{\sigma}{|\sigma|}\ .

(3.43)

These signs agree with the known universal solution
[
50
,
189
]
near the AdS boundary, at
\sigma\to\infty
, and they ensure that walls entering the ergoregion have no kink. Expressing the denominators in terms of the horizon locations (
3.52
) gives the equations (
3.50
) and (
3.51
) of the main text.

It is worth noting that the tensionless (
\lambda\to 0
) limit of our solution is singular. Indeed, on one hand, extremising the brane action and ignoring its back-reaction gives a geodesic worldvolume, but on the other hand for
\lambda=0
fluctuations of the string are unsuppressed. In fact, when
\lambda
is small the wall starts as a geodesic near the AdS boundary but always departs significantly in the interior.

The results of sec.
3.4
can be summarised as follows. First, from (
3.23
)

\displaystyle r_{j}(\sigma)=\sqrt{\sigma+M_{j}\ell_{j}^{\,2}}\ .

(3.44)

Secondly, we find a sufficient and necessary condition on the parameters for the existence of solutions :

\displaystyle J_{1}=-J_{2}

(3.45)

This ensures conservation of energy in the CFT, as seen from the holographic dictionary (
3.7
). Thirdly, matching
h_{\tau\sigma}
from the two sides determines the time delay in terms of the embedding functions
x_{j}
,

\displaystyle\Delta t^{\prime}\equiv f_{2}^{\prime}-f_{1}^{\prime}=\frac{J_{1}}{2\sigma}(\ell_{1}x_{1}^{\prime}+\ell_{2}x_{2}^{\prime})\ ,

(3.46)

where primes denote derivatives with respect to
\sigma
. What remains is thus to find the functions
x_{j}(\sigma)
.

To this end, we use the continuity of
h_{\sigma\sigma}
and the
\tau\tau
component of (
2.18
). It is useful and convenient to first solve these two equations for the determinant of the induced metric, with the result

\displaystyle-{\rm det}\hat{g}=\frac{\lambda^{2}\sigma}{{A\sigma^{2}+2B\sigma+C}}=\frac{\lambda^{2}\sigma}{{A(\sigma-\sigma_{+})(\sigma-\sigma_{-})}}\ ,

(3.47)

where

\displaystyle\sigma_{\pm}=\frac{-B\pm\sqrt{B^{2}-AC}}{A}\ ,

(3.48)

and the coefficients
A,B,C
read

\displaystyle A=(\lambda_{\max}^{2}-\lambda^{2})(\lambda^{2}-\lambda_{\min}^{2})\ ,

\displaystyle B=\lambda^{2}(M_{1}+M_{2})-\lambda_{0}^{2}(M_{1}-M_{2})\ ,

(3.49)

\displaystyle C=-(M_{1}-M_{2})^{2}+\lambda^{2}J_{1}^{2}\ .

The three critical tensions entering the above coefficients have been defined in the previous chapter, sec. (
2.4
).

\lambda_{\rm min}=\biggl|\frac{1}{\ell_{1}}-\frac{1}{\ell_{2}}\biggl|\hskip 10.00002pt\lambda_{\rm max}=\frac{1}{\ell_{1}}+\frac{1}{\ell_{2}}\hskip 10.00002pt\lambda_{0}=\sqrt{\lambda_{\rm max}\lambda_{\rm min}}\ .

(
2.30
)

Without loss of generality we assume, as earlier, that
\ell_{1}\leq\ell_{2}
, so the absolute value in
\lambda_{\rm min}
is superfluous. Note that the expressions (
3.47
) to (
3.49
) are the same as the ones for static branes, see (
2.35
), except for the extra term
\lambda^{2}J_{1}^{2}
in the coefficient
C
.

The determinant of the induced metric can be expressed in terms of
x_{j}
and
\sigma
in each chart,
j=1
and
j=2
. It does not depend on the time-shift functions
f_{j}
, which could be absorbed by a reparametrization of the metric with unit Jacobian. Having already extracted
{\rm det}h
, one can now invert these relations to find the
x_{j}^{\prime}
,

\displaystyle\frac{x_{1}^{\prime}}{\ell_{1}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{{\rm sgn}(\sigma)\big[(\lambda^{2}+\lambda_{0}^{2})\sigma^{2}+(M_{1}-M_{2})\sigma\big]}{2(\sigma-\sigma_{+}^{\rm H1})(\sigma-\sigma_{-}^{\rm H1})\sqrt{A\sigma(\sigma-\sigma_{+})(\sigma-\sigma_{-})}}\ ,

(3.50)

\displaystyle\frac{x_{2}^{\prime}}{\ell_{2}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{{\rm sgn}(\sigma)\big[(\lambda^{2}-\lambda_{0}^{2})\,\sigma^{2}\,-(M_{1}-M_{2})\sigma\big]}{2(\sigma-\sigma_{+}^{\rm H2})(\sigma-\sigma_{-}^{\rm H2})\sqrt{A\sigma(\sigma-\sigma_{+})(\sigma-\sigma_{-})}}\ ,

(3.51)

where we denoted the horizons locations in the blueshift parametrization:

\displaystyle\sigma_{\pm}^{{\rm H}j}=-\frac{M_{j}\ell_{j}^{2}}{2}\pm\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{M_{j}^{2}\ell_{j}^{4}-J_{j}^{2}\ell_{j}^{2}}\ .

(3.52)

These points are where the outer and inner horizons of the
j
th BTZ metric intersect the domain wall.

Eqs. (
3.44
) to (
3.52
) give the general stationary solution of the thin-brane equations for any Lagrangian parameters
\ell_{j}
and
\lambda
, and geometric parameters
M_{j}
and
J_{1}=-J_{2}
. The Lagrangian parameters are part of the basic data of the interface CFT, while the geometric parameters determine the CFT state. When
J_{1}=J_{2}=0
, all these expressions reduce to the static solutions described in chap.
2
.

3.5
Inside the ergoregion

The qualitative behaviour of the domain wall is governed by the singularities of (
3.50
,
3.51
), as one moves from the AdS boundary at
\sigma\sim\infty
inwards. In addition to the BTZ horizons at
\sigma^{{\rm H}j}_{\pm}
, other potential singularities arise at
\sigma_{\pm}
and at the entrance of the ergoregion
\sigma=0
. From (
3.47
) we see that the brane worldvolume would become spacelike beyond
\sigma=0
, if
\sigma_{\pm}
are both either negative or complex.

To avoid such pathological behaviour one of the following two conditions must be met:

•

\,\sigma_{+}>0\,:
The singularity at
\sigma_{+}
is in this case a turning point, and the

wall does not extend to lower values of
\sigma
. Indeed, as seen from (
3.50
) and (
3.51
), the singularity as
\sigma_{+}
is integrable.

•

\,0=\sigma_{+}>\sigma_{-}\,:
In this case the worldvolume remains timelike as the wall enters the ergoregion. The reader can verify from eqs. (
3.47
), (
3.50
) and (
3.51
) that the embedding near
\sigma=0
is smooth.

Figure 3.4
:
The two kinds of stationary-wall geometries: (i) The wall avoids the ergoregion, turns around and intersects the AdS boundary twice; or (ii) it enters the ergoregion and does not come out again. The broken line is the ergoplane, the two outer BTZ regions are coloured in green and pink, and the region behind the horizon in grey. The horizon in case (ii) will be described in detail in the coming section.

These two possibilities are illustrated in figure
3.4
. Branes entering the ergoregion are dual, as will become clear, to steady states of an isolated interface, while those that avoid the ergoregion are dual to steady states of an interface anti-interface pair. We will return to the second case in section
3.7
, here we focus on the isolated interface.

The condition
\sigma_{+}=0
implies
C=0
and
B\geq 0
. Using (
3.48
) and (
3.49
), and the fact that the coefficient
A
is positive for tensions in the allowable range (
\lambda_{\min}<\lambda<\lambda_{\max}
) we obtain

\displaystyle M_{1}-M_{2}=\pm\lambda J_{1}=\mp\lambda J_{2}\hskip 10.00002pt{\rm and}\hskip 10.00002pt\lambda^{2}(M_{1}+M_{2})\geq\lambda_{0}^{\,2}(M_{1}-M_{2})\ .

(3.53)

Furthermore, cosmic censorship requires that
\ell_{j}M_{j}>|J_{j}|
unless the bulk singularity at
r_{j}=0
is excised (this is the case in the pink region of the left fig.
3.4
. If none of the singularities is excised, the inequality in (
3.53
) is automatically satisfied and hence redundant.

With the help of the holographic dictionary (
3.7
) one can translate the expression (
3.53
) for
M_{1}-M_{2}
to the language of ICFT. Since the incoming fluxes are thermal,
T_{--}^{(j)}=\pi^{2}\ell_{j}\Theta_{j}^{2}
and (
3.7
) gives

\displaystyle M_{j}={4\pi^{2}\Theta_{j}^{2}}-\frac{J_{j}}{\ell_{j}}\Longrightarrow\ ,M_{1}-M_{2}=4\pi^{2}(\Theta_{1}^{2}-\Theta_{2}^{2})-J_{1}(\frac{1}{\ell_{1}}+\frac{1}{\ell_{2}})\ .

(3.54)

Combining with (
3.53
) gives the heat-flow rate

\displaystyle J_{1}=2\langle T^{(1)\,tx}\rangle={4\pi^{2}}\big[\frac{1}{\ell_{1}}+\frac{1}{\ell_{2}}\pm\lambda\big]^{-1}(\Theta_{1}^{2}-\Theta_{2}^{2})\ .

(3.55)

This agrees with the ICFT expression (
3.15
) if we identify the transmission coefficients as follows (recall that
c_{j}=12\pi\ell_{j}
)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{T}_{j}}=\frac{2}{\ell_{j}}\big[\frac{1}{\ell_{1}}+\frac{1}{\ell_{2}}\pm\lambda\big]^{-1}\ .

(3.56)

It is gratifying to find that, for the choice of plus sign, (
3.56
) are precisely the coefficients
{\mathcal{T}_{j}}
computed in the linearized approximation in ref.
[
24
]
. In essence, this is a non-perturbative derivation of the transmission coefficients of the interface in the minimal model. The correct choice of sign will be justified in a minute.

Let us pause here to take stock of the situation. We found that (i) the dual of an isolated interface
must
correspond to a brane that enters the ergoregion (otherwise it will turn back, and the dual will contain two interfaces), and (ii) that the brane equations determine in this case the flow of heat in accordance with the CFT result of
[
133
,
51
]
and the transmission coefficients found in
[
24
]
.
8
8

8

The fact that our non-linear analysis agrees with the linearized-wave treatment of
[
24
]
is an indirect confirmation of the fact that the transport coefficients are universal.

To complete the story, we must make sure that once inside the ergoregion the brane does not come out again. If it did, it would intersect the AdS boundary at a second point, so the solution would not be dual to an isolated interface as claimed.

Inserting
\sigma_{+}=0
in the embedding functions (
3.50
,
3.51
) we find

\displaystyle\frac{x_{1}^{\prime}}{\ell_{1}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{(\lambda^{2}+\lambda_{0}^{2})\sigma+(M_{1}-M_{2})}{2(\sigma-\sigma_{+}^{\rm H1})(\sigma-\sigma_{-}^{\rm H1})\sqrt{A(\sigma-\sigma_{-})}}\ ,

(3.57)

\displaystyle\frac{x_{2}^{\prime}}{\ell_{2}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{(\lambda^{2}-\lambda_{0}^{2})\sigma-(M_{1}-M_{2})}{2(\sigma-\sigma_{+}^{\rm H2})(\sigma-\sigma_{-}^{\rm H2})\sqrt{A(\sigma-\sigma_{-})}}\ ,

where the
\sigma_{\pm}^{{\rm H}j}
are given by (
3.28
) and

\displaystyle\sigma_{-}=-\frac{2\lambda}{A}\big[\lambda(M_{1}+M_{2})\pm 2\lambda_{0}^{2}J_{1}\big]\ .

(3.58)

As already said, the embedding is regular at
\sigma=0
, i.e. the brane enters the ergoregion smoothly. What it does next depends on which singularity it encounters first. If this were the square-root singularity at
\sigma_{-}
, the wall would turn around (just like it does for positive
\sigma_{+}
), exit the ergoregion and intersect the AdS boundary at another anchor point. This is the possibility that we want to exclude.

Consider for starters the simpler case
\ell_{1}=\ell_{2}\equiv\ell
. In this case
\lambda_{0}=0
and
A=\lambda^{2}(4/\ell^{2}-\lambda^{2})
, so (
3.58
) reduces to

\displaystyle\sigma_{-}=-\frac{2\ell^{2}(M_{1}+M_{2})}{4-\lambda^{2}\ell^{2}}\leq-{\rm min}(M_{j})\ell^{2}\ .

(3.59)

In the last step we used the fact that both
M_{j}
are positive, otherwise the conical singularity at
r_{j}=0\Longleftrightarrow\sigma=-M_{j}\ell^{2}
would be naked.

What (
3.59
) shows is that the putative turning point
\sigma_{-}
lies behind the bulk singularity in at least one of the two BTZ regions, where our solution cannot be extended. Thus this turning point is never reached.

For general
\ell_{1}\not=\ell_{2}
a weaker statement is true, namely that
\sigma_{-}
is shielded by an inner horizon for at least one
j
. For a proof, we maximize
\sigma_{-}
with respect to the brane tension
\lambda
. We have performed this calculation with Mathematica, but do not find it useful to reproduce details here. The key point for our purposes is that there are no solutions in which the brane enters the ergoregion, turns around before an inner horizon, and exits towards the AdS boundary. Since as argued by Penrose
[
191
]
, Cauchy (inner) horizons are classically unstable,
9
9

9

For recent discussions of strong cosmic censorship in the BTZ black hole see
[
192
,
193
,
194
,
195
,
196
]
.
solutions in which the turning point lies behind one of them cannot be trusted. As such, the cauchy horizon should be viewed as the singularities of the black hole.

One last remark is in order concerning the induced brane metric
h_{ab}
. By redefining the worldvolume time,
\tilde{\tau}=\tau+{J\ell_{1}}\int{x_{1}^{\prime}(\sigma)}d\sigma/{2\sigma}
, we can bring this metric to the diagonal form :

d\hat{s}^{2}=-\sigma d{\tilde{\tau}}^{2}+|{\rm det}\,\hat{g}|\,\frac{d\sigma^{2}}{\sigma}\hskip 20.00003pt{\rm with}\hskip 20.00003pt{\rm det}\,\hat{g}=\frac{\lambda^{2}}{{A(\sigma_{-}-\sigma)}}\ .

(3.60)

The worldvolume is timelike for all
\sigma>\sigma_{-}
, as already advertised. More interestingly, the metric (felt by signals that propagate on the brane) is that of a two-dimensional black-hole with horizon at the ergoplane
\sigma=0
. This lies outside the bulk horizons
\sigma_{+}^{{\rm H}j}
, in agreement with arguments showing that the causal structure is always set by the Einstein metric
[
197
]
. Similar remarks in a closely-related context were made before in ref.
[
198
]
.

The brane-horizon (bH) temperature,

\displaystyle 4\pi\Theta_{\rm bH}=\big(-{\rm det}\,\hat{g}|_{\sigma=0}\big)^{-1/2}\ ,

(3.61)

is intermediate between
\Theta_{1}
and
\Theta_{2}
as can be easily checked. For
\ell_{1}=\ell_{2}
for example one finds
2\,\Theta_{\rm bH}^{2}=\Theta_{1}^{2}+\Theta_{2}^{2}
.

3.6
The non-Killing horizon

Since
\sigma_{-}
lies behind an inner horizon, the first singularities of the embedding functions (
3.50
,
3.51
) are at
\sigma_{+}^{{\rm H}j}
. A key feature of the non-static solutions is that these outer BTZ horizons, which are apparent horizons as will become clear, do not meet at the same point on the brane.
For
J_{j}\not=0
the following strict inequalities indeed hold

\sigma_{+}^{{\rm H}1}>\sigma_{+}^{{\rm H}2}\hskip 10.00002pt{\rm if}\ \ M_{1}>M_{2}\hskip 20.00003pt\sigma_{+}^{{\rm H}2}<\sigma_{+}^{{\rm H}1}\hskip 10.00002pt{\rm if}\ \ M_{1}<M_{2}\ .

(3.62)

For small
J_{j}
these inequalities are manifest by Taylor expanding (
3.52
),

\sigma_{+}^{{\rm H}j}=-\frac{J_{j}^{2}}{M_{j}}+O(J_{j}^{\,4})\ .

(3.63)

We show that they hold for all
J_{j}
in appendix
C.2
.

The meaning of these inequalities becomes clear if we use the holographic dictionary (
3.7
), the energy currents (
3.12
) and the detailed-balance condition (
3.13
) to write the
M_{j}
as follows

\displaystyle

\displaystyle M_{1}=2\pi^{2}\left[\Theta_{1}^{2}(1+{\mathcal{R}}_{1})+\Theta_{2}^{2}(1-{\mathcal{R}}_{1})\right]\ ,

(3.64)

\displaystyle M_{2}=2\pi^{2}\left[\Theta_{1}^{2}(1-{\mathcal{R}}_{2})+\Theta_{2}^{2}(1+{\mathcal{R}}_{2})\right]\ .

Assuming
0\leq{\mathcal{R}}_{j}\leq 1
, we see that the hotter side of the interface has the larger
M_{j}
. What (
3.62
) therefore says is that the brane hits the BTZ horizon of the hotter side first.

3.6.1
The arrow of time

Assume for concreteness
M_{1}>M_{2}
, the case
M_{2}>M_{1}
being similar.
10
10

10

Strictly speaking we also ask that the brane hits both outer horizons before the inner (Cauchy) horizons, since we cannot trust our classical solutions beyond the latter. As explained in appendix
C.2
, this condition is automatic when
M_{2}>M_{1}
, but not when
M_{1}>M_{2}
, where it is possible for some range of parameters to have
\sigma_{+}^{{\rm H}2}<\sigma_{-}^{{\rm H}1}
. Those specific solutions should be discarded, although we do not think that this difference has a deeper physical meaning.

From eq.(
3.53
) we have
M_{1}=M_{2}+\lambda|J_{1}|
. We do not commit yet on the sign of
J_{1}
, nor on the sign in (
3.53
), but the product of the two should be positive. From the holographic interpretation, we expect
J_{1}
to be positive (heat flows from the hot to the cold side), so the correct sign in (
3.53
) is plus (to match the correct reflection/transmission coefficients), but we would like to understand how this condition arises from the gravity side.

Figure
3.5
shows a sketch of the behaviour of the brane past the ergoplane. The vertical axis is parameterised by
\sigma
(increasing downwards), and the horizontal axes by the ingoing Eddington-Finkelstein coordinates
y_{j}
defined in (
3.4
). These coordinates are regular at the future horizons, and reduce to the flat ICFT coordinates
x_{j}
at the AdS boundary. Therefore, they do not affect the CFT state, or in other words, they are pure gauge and act trivially on the asymptotic boundary.

Figure 3.5
:
A brane (thick black curve) entering the local outer horizons
{\mathcal{H}}1
and
{\mathcal{H}}2
(the boundaries of the grey regions in the figure) at two different points E1 and E2. The piece [E1,E2] of the wall is behind the horizon of slice 1 but outside the horizon of slice 2. The thick orange arrows show the direction of heat flow. The white curve is the worldline of an observer entering
{\mathcal{H}}1
, crossing the brane and emerging outside
{\mathcal{H}}2
.

Let us take a closer look at the wall embedding in Eddington-Finkelstein (EF) coordinates. From (
3.50
,
3.51
) and the identities
r_{j}^{\prime}=1/2r_{j}
we get

\displaystyle

\displaystyle y_{1}^{\prime}=\frac{\ell_{1}}{2(\sigma-\sigma_{+}^{\rm H1})(\sigma-\sigma_{-}^{\rm H1})}\left[\frac{J_{1}\ell_{1}}{2\sqrt{\sigma+M_{1}\ell_{1}^{2}}}-\frac{(\lambda^{2}+\lambda_{0}^{2})\sigma+\lambda|J_{1}|}{\sqrt{A(\sigma-\sigma_{-})}}\right]\ ,

(3.65)

\displaystyle y_{2}^{\prime}=\frac{\ell_{2}}{2(\sigma-\sigma_{+}^{\rm H2})(\sigma-\sigma_{-}^{\rm H2})}\left[\frac{J_{2}\ell_{2}}{2\sqrt{\sigma+M_{2}\ell_{2}^{2}}}-\frac{(\lambda^{2}-\lambda_{0}^{2})\sigma-\lambda|J_{2}|}{\sqrt{A(\sigma-\sigma_{-})}}\right]\ .

A little algebra shows that the square brackets in the above expression vanish at the corresponding horizons
\sigma=\sigma_{+}^{{\rm H}j}
if
J_{1}=-J_{2}>0
. The functions
y_{j}
present no singularity at the horizon. By contrast, if
J_{1}=-J_{2}<0
these functions are singular:
y_{1}\to+\infty
at
\sigma_{+}^{{\rm H}1}
, and
y_{2}\to-\infty
at
\sigma_{+}^{{\rm H}2}
.

Consider first the case
J_{1}>0
. At first, it seems remarkable that in Eddigton-Finkelstein coordinates, the singularity at the horizon
\sigma=\sigma_{+}^{{\rm H}j}
disappears and the brane enters smoothly in the black hole. After all, the membrane depends on
\lambda
,
J_{1}
and both
M_{j}
, while the Eddington-Finkelstein coordinates change depends only on one side’s parameters,
M_{j}
,
J_{j}
. Indeed, there are seemingly ”miraculous” cancellations at play at
\sigma_{+}^{{\rm H}j}
so that the behavior of the membrane at this location is independent on the tension. This can somehow be explained by remembering that the Eddington-Finkelstein coordinates are adapted to infalling observers. It appears that close to the horizon, the shape of the membrane is independent of its tension, and its shape follows a falling observer’s trajectory.

In the case
J_{1}<0
, this cancellation of course does not happen, and the membrane does not enter the future horizon. However, if we go to outgoing E-F coordinates, we find that the membrane exits smoothly the past horizon of the white hole.

Knowing this, we now understand why we find a pair of solutions dual to the stationary ICFT system. The first one, with
J_{1}>0
generates a heat flow in the correct direction in the field theory; from hot to cold. In this case,
M_{1}=M_{2}+\lambda J_{1}
, and hence the sign in the expression (
3.56
) for the transmission coefficients is plus, in agreement with the result of ref.
[
24
]
.

The other solution with
J_{1}<0
can be obtained by time-reversal, which leaves the
M_{j}
unchanged. This gives an explanation for the interpretation of this companion solution on the field theory: it is the same stationary state, but time-inverted. Indeed, if one chooses the minus sign in the expressions (
3.56
) (which is the ”incorrect” transmission coefficients), and plugs them in (
3.12
), while at the same time exchanging
T_{--}\leftrightarrow T_{++}
, one will recover the equations (
3.12
), but now with the ”correct” transmission and reflections coefficients. Indeed, in the time-reversed stationary states, the ”incident” rays will be the left-movers. Thus, the minus sign of (
3.56
) appears when one chooses the incorrect time direction for the stationary states, and as such, misinterprets the scattering experiment.

In the end, both solutions describe exactly the same situation. By choosing the time direction to be forward, we will have to discard the time-reversed solution. In gravity, similarly to a white hole, which solves Einstein’s equations but cannot be produced by gravitational collapse, we expect that no physical protocol can prepare the
J_{1}<0
solution.

3.6.2
Event versus apparent horizon

Denote by
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{H}}_{2}
the horizons of the two BTZ regions of the stationary geometry, and by
E
1
and
E
2
their intersections with the brane worldvolume. We can foliate spacetime by Cauchy slices
v_{j}=\bar{v}+\epsilon_{j}(r_{j},x_{j})
, where
\bar{v}
is a uniform foliation parameter.
11
11

11

The non-trivial radial dependence in the definition of the Cauchy slice is necessary because constant
v_{j}
curves are lightlike behind the
j
th horizon.
We use the same symbols for the projections of
{\mathcal{H}}_{j}
and
E
j
on a Cauchy slice. Since simultaneous translations of
v_{j}
are Killing isometries, the projections do not depend on
\bar{v}
.

Both
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{H}}_{2}
are local (or apparent) horizons, i.e. future-directed light rays can only traverse them in one direction. But it is clear from figure
3.5
that
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
cannot be part of the event horizon of global spacetime. Indeed, after entering
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
an observer moving to the right can traverse the [
E
1
,
E
2
] part of the wall, emerge outside
{\mathcal{H}}_{2}
in region 2, and from there continue her journey to the boundary. Such journeys are only forbidden if
E
1
=
E
2
,
i.e. for the static equilibrium solutions.

Before proceeding, let us briefly describe an alternative route that was considered before arriving at the correct resolution, since we believe it might be interesting to some readers. In our initial attempt, we searched for a way to prevent an observer from doing the trip depicted in white in (
3.5
), keeping the apparent horizon as event horizons. A way to do that is to clip the membrane at
E1
. This is of course already not very satisfying on side 1, as the membrane terminates on the horizon, but we hoped there might be a way to justify this.

On side 2, this is even harder to justify, as we would have a ”dangling” membrane, and it seems that an observer could go around the membrane and cross it from the ”wrong side”, which gives a picture of the bulk which is completely different. This new problem can be resolved thanks to the ergosphere. Indeed, no observer could make the trip to go behind
E_{1}
from the pink
side, because the dragging forces would prevent him. There would thus be an artificial ”end of the world”, as no observer could pass behind the membrane. Incidentally, this also worked as an explanation for the correct sign choice of the currents
J_{i}
, as inverting them spoils this setup. Ultimately, this explanation was discarded, as there was no way to explain the abrupt ending of the membranes without introducing additional matter fields, and while for observers the geometry was consistent, the full geometry of the spacetime was quite strange, as with a spacelike trajectory one could go ”around” the membrane. As we explain now, the real resolution is that the event horizon is deformed by the gluing of the spacetimes.

In order to analyze the problem systematically, we define an everywhere-timelike unit vector field that will define the arrow of time in the full spacetime.

t^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}=\frac{\partial}{\partial v_{j}}+\frac{h_{j}(r_{j})-1}{2\ell_{j}}\frac{\partial}{\partial r_{j}}+\frac{J_{j}\ell_{j}}{2r_{j}^{2}}\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{j}}\hskip 20.00003pt{\rm in\ the\ {\it j}th\ region\,.}\ .

(3.66)

Using the metric (
3.5
) the reader can check that
t^{\mu}t_{\mu}=-1
. To avoid charging the formulae we drop temporarily the index
j
. A future-directed null curve has a tangent vector

\dot{x}^{\mu}=(\dot{v},\dot{r},\dot{y})\hskip 20.00003pt{\rm where}\hskip 10.00002pt\dot{x}^{\mu}\dot{x}_{\mu}=0\ \ \ {\rm and}\ \ \ \dot{x}^{\mu}t_{\mu}<0\ .

(3.67)

The dots denote derivatives with respect to a parameter on the curve. Solving the conditions (
3.67
) gives

\dot{r}=\frac{h}{2\ell}\dot{v}-\frac{r^{2}}{2\ell\dot{v}}\bigl(\dot{y}-\frac{J\ell}{2r^{2}}\dot{v}\bigr)^{2}\ \hskip 10.00002pt{\rm and}\ \ \ \dot{v}>0\ .

(3.68)

We see that the arrow of time is defined by increasing
v
, and that behind the horizon, where
h(r)
is negative,
r
is monotone decreasing with time, meaning a causal curve necessarily advances deeper into the black hole. This suffices to show that
{\mathcal{H}}_{2}

is
part of the event horizon – an observer crossing it will never make it out to the boundary again, and will plummet to the Cauchy Horizon. Crossing the membrane does not help, as the observer finds himself behind
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
, where he continues to plummet.

As already shown, the story differs in region 1, as an observer may follow a curve similar to the one depicted in (
3.5
). Here, the event horizon consists of a lightlike surface
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
such that no future-directed causal curve starting from a point behind it can reach the [
E
1
,
E
2
] part of the wall. Indeed, once a causal curve enters behind
\sigma^{\rm H1}_{+}
, it necessarily must sink in further because of (
3.68
). Its only chance of escape is reaching the wall at [
E
1
,
E
2
], and thus if this is not possible, it is indeed located behind the event horizon.

Clearly, the full event horizon

{\mathcal{H}}_{\rm event}=\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{1}\,\cup\,{\mathcal{H}}_{2}

(3.69)

must be continuous and lie behind the apparent horizon
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
in region 1. This is illustrated in fig.
3.6
. General theorems
[
167
]
actually show that a local horizon which is part of a trapped compact surface cannot lie outside the event horizon. But there is no clash with these theorems here because
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
fails to be compact, both at infinity and at
E
1
.

Figure 3.6
:
The event and apparent horizons,
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{1}\cup{\mathcal{H}}_{2}
and
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}\cup{\mathcal{H}}_{2}
, as described in the text. The event horizon is connected but it is not Killing. Projections of the local light-cone on a Cauchy slice are shown in yellow. The light grey region behind
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
is outside the event horizon because signals can escape towards the right.

To compute the projection of
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
on a Cauchy slice, note first that it is a curve that passes through the point
E
2
. When a causal curve enters the apparent horizon
\sigma^{\rm H1}_{+}
, it will inexorably sink deeper into it. Its best chance at escaping the singularity it thus to maximize its speed toward the wall. The constraints on the tangent vector will be less stringent when the curve is lightlike.

Time-reversing this thought process, it is easy to convince oneself that the projection of the horizon will be the curve that is everywhere tangent to the projection of the local light cone, as shown in fig.
3.6
. Put differently, at every point on the curve we must minimize the angle between (the projection of) light-like vectors and the positive-
y_{1}
axis. This will guarantee that an observer starting behind
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
will not be able to move fast enough towards the right in order to hit the wall before the point
E
2
.

Parametrising the curve by
y_{1}
, using (
3.68
) and dropping again for simplicity the
j=1
index we find

\displaystyle-\frac{dy}{dr}\,\bigg|_{\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{1}}\ =\ {\rm max}_{\,v_{y}>0}\ \bigg[\frac{r^{2}}{2\ell v_{y}}\bigl(1-\frac{J\ell}{2r^{2}}{v_{y}}\bigr)^{2}-\frac{h}{2\ell}{v_{y}}\bigg]^{-1}\ ,

(3.70)

where
v_{y}\equiv dv/dy
. The extrema of this expression are
v_{y}=\pm r/\sqrt{M\ell^{2}-r^{2}}
. Recall that we are interested in the region behind the BTZ horizon and in future-directed light rays for which
v
is monotone increasing (whereas
r
is monotone decreasing). For null rays moving to the right we should thus pick the positive
v_{y}
extremum.
Inserting in (
3.70
) gives the differential equation obeyed by
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
,

\displaystyle\frac{dy}{dr}\bigg|_{\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}1}=\frac{2\ell}{J\ell-2r\sqrt{M\ell^{2}-r^{2}}}\ .

(3.71)

The (projected) event horizon in region
j=1
is the integral of (
3.71
) with the constant of integration fixed so that the curve passes through
E
2
.

Here now comes the important point. The reader can check that near the BTZ horizon,
r=r_{+}^{\rm H1}(1+\epsilon)
with
\epsilon\ll 1
, the denominator in (
3.71
) vanishes like
\epsilon
. This is a non-integrable singularity, so
y(r)
diverges at
r_{+}^{\rm H1}
and hence
{\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}1}
approaches asymptotically
{\mathcal{H}}1
as announced in section
3.2.2
. Presumably, the holographic entropy will therefore asymptote to that of the equilibrium BTZ horizon, given by (
3.11
). This suggests that the chiral outgoing fluid is thermal, not only in the cold region 2 but also in the hotter region 1, at least far from the interface. But since the state of the outgoing fluid will be the same on the full slice, we conclude that it is thermal everywhere. Many questions remain about this deformed event horizon, particularly whether it is indeed related to the entropy of the black hole. Near the interface, the HRT surface measuring the entanglement entropy will certainly be deformed, but it is unclear if it will enter the apparent horizon or not. These questions will be explored in the next chapter.

3.6.3
Remark on flowing funnels

The fact that outgoing fluxes are thermalized means that, in what concerns the entropy and energy flows, the interface behaves like a black cavity. This latter can be modeled by a non-dynamical, two-sided boundary black hole whose (disconnected) horizon consists of two points. To mimic the behaviour of the interface, the two horizon temperatures should be equal to the
\Theta_{j}^{\rm eff}
that saturate the bounds (
3.21
). This is illustrated in figure
3.7
.

Figure 3.7
:
A two-sided flowing funnel that can mimic the energy and entropy flows of the holographic interface. Tuning the horizon temperatures so that the boundary black hole does not absorb any energy is, however, an adhoc condition.

The precise shape of the flowing horizon(s) depends on the boundary black hole(s) and is not important for our purposes here. For completeness, following ref.
[
170
]
, we outline how to derive it
in appendix
C.3
. Like the thin-brane horizon of figure
3.6
, it approaches the BTZ horizons at infinity but differs in the central region (notably with a delta-function peak in the entropy density at
x=0
, see app.
C.3
).

The key difference is however elsewhere. The two halves of the flowing funnel of figure
3.7
are a priori separate solutions, with the temperatures
\Theta_{j}
and
\Theta_{j}^{\rm eff}
chosen at will. To mimic the conformal interface one must impose continuity of the heat flow,

\frac{dQ_{1}}{dt}=\frac{\pi c_{1}}{12}(\Theta_{1}^{2}-(\Theta_{1}^{\rm eff})^{2})=\frac{\pi c_{2}}{12}((\Theta_{2}^{\rm eff})^{2}-\Theta_{2}^{2})=\frac{dQ_{2}}{dt}\ .

(3.72)

This relates the horizon temperatures to each other and to those of the distant heat baths. It is however unclear whether any local condition behind the event horizons can impose the condition (
3.72
).

3.7
Pair of interfaces

In this last section we consider a pair of identical interfaces between two theories, CFT
1
and CFT
2
.
12
12

12

Our branes are not oriented, so there is no difference between an interface and anti-interface. More general setups could include several different CFTs and triple junctions of branes, but such systems are beyond the scope of the present work.

The interface separation is
\Delta x
. Let the theory that lives in the finite interval be CFT
2
and the theory outside be CFT
1
(recall that we are assuming
\ell_{2}\geq\ell_{1}
). At thermal equilibrium the system undergoes a first-order phase transition at a critical temperature
\Theta_{\rm cr}=b/\Delta x
where
b
depends on the classical Lagrangian parameters
\lambda\ell_{j}

[
189
,
77
]
.

Below
\Theta_{\rm cr}
the brane avoids the horizon and is connected, while above
\Theta_{\rm cr}
it breaks into two disjoint pieces that hit separately the singularity of the black hole. This is a variant of the Hawking-Page phase transition
[
23
]
that can be interpreted
[
55
]
as a deconfinement transition of CFT
2
.

We would like to understand what happens when this system is coupled to reservoirs with slightly different temperatures
\Theta_{\pm}=\Theta\pm d\Theta
at
x=\pm\infty
. Because of the temperature gradient, the branes are now stationary, but they conserve the topology of their static ancestors. In the low-
\Theta
phase the brane avoids the ergoregion (which is displaced from the horizon infinitesimally) and stays connected, while in the high-
\Theta
phase it splits in two disjoint branes that enter the ergoregion and hit separately a Cauchy horizon or a bulk singularity.

The two phases are illustrated in figure
3.8
.

Figure 3.8
:
The two types of NESS for an interface pair. In the ’quantum phase’ (left)heat conducts as if there was no scatterer, while in the ’classical phase’ (right) the conductance is the same as for an isolated CFT
1
defect dual to a brane of tension
2\lambda
. Also given in the figure is the BTZ mass parameter in different regions of the geometry. The yellow arrows show the direction of heat flow.

Consider the high-
\Theta
phase first. The isolated-brane solution of sections
3.5
and
3.6
is here juxtaposed to a solution in which the roles of CFT
1
and CFT
2
are inverted. The mass parameter of the three BTZ regions decreases in the direction of heat flow, jumping by
\lambda J
across each brane.
This is indeed the ‘ticket of entry’ to the ergoregion, as explained in eq. (
3.53
) and section
3.6.1
.

The total change of BTZ mass across the pair is the same as if the two branes had merged into a single one with twice the tension. Using the holographic dictionary (
3.7
) and the fact that incoming fluxes at
x=\pm\infty
are thermal with temperatures
\Theta_{\pm}
one indeed computes

\underline{{\rm high}\ \Theta}:\hskip 20.00003pt\frac{dQ}{dt}=\frac{\pi^{2}\ell_{1}}{1+\lambda\ell_{1}}(\Theta^{2}_{-}-\Theta^{2}_{+})\equiv{\pi^{2}\ell_{1}}{\mathcal{T}}_{\rm pair}(\Theta^{2}_{-}-\Theta^{2}_{+})\ ,

(3.73)

where the effective transmission coefficient
{\mathcal{T}}_{\rm pair}
is that of a CFT
1
defect whose dual brane has tension
2\lambda
. Note in passing that this effective brane tension can exceed the upper bound (
2.30
) above which an individual brane inflates, and that an array of widely-spaced branes can make the transmission coefficient arbitrarily small.

The heat flow (
3.73
) is what one would obtain from classical scatterers.
13
13

13

The argument grew out of a conversation with Giuseppe Policastro who noticed that the tensions of two juxtaposed branes effectively add up in the calculation of ref.
[
24
]

To understand why, think of
{\mathcal{T}}_{j}
and
{\mathcal{R}}_{j}
as classical transmission and reflection probabilities for quasi-particles incident on the interface from the side
j
. The probability of passing through both interfaces is the sum of probabilities of trajectories with any number of double reflections in between,

{\mathcal{T}}_{\rm pair}={\mathcal{T}}_{1}(1+{\mathcal{R}}_{2}^{2}+{\mathcal{R}}_{2}^{4}+\cdots){\mathcal{T}}_{2}=\frac{{\mathcal{T}}_{1}{\mathcal{T}}_{2}}{1-{\mathcal{R}}_{2}^{2}}=\frac{1}{1+\ell_{1}\lambda}\ ,

(3.74)

where in the last step we used the holographic relations (
2.32
). This gives precisely the result (
3.73
) as advertised.

The low-
\Theta
case is drastically different. The solution is now obtained by gluing a brane with a turning point (i.e.
\sigma_{+}>0
, see section
3.3.2
) to its mirror image, so that the brane has reflection symmetry. The bulk metric, however, is not
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
symmetric because in the mirror image we do not flip the sign of the BTZ ‘spin’
J
. This is required for the continuity of the
dx/dt
component of the bulk metric. While it may seem we are gluing brane with its time-reversed counterpart this is not the case here. The arguments that lead to this conclusion in sec.
3.6.2
relied on the regularity at the horizon, which is absent here. Similarly, the argument from the transmission coefficients in the field theory are not applicable here, since there might be interference effects on the energy fluxes in the middle of the two membranes. The BTZ mass is thus the same at
x=\pm\infty
, while its value in the CFT
2
region depends on the interface separation
\Delta x
. It follows from the holographic dictionary (
3.7
) that the heat flow is in this case unobstructed,

\underline{{\rm low}\Theta}:\hskip 20.00003pt\frac{dQ}{dt}={\pi^{2}\ell_{1}}(\Theta^{2}_{-}-\Theta^{2}_{+})\ ,

(3.75)

i.e. the effective transmission coefficient is
{\mathcal{T}}_{\rm pair}=1
. Superficially, it looks as if two branes with equal and opposite tensions have merged into a tensionless one.

In reality, however, the above phenomenon is deeply quantum. What the calculation says is that when a characteristic thermal wavelength becomes larger than the interface separation, coherent scattering results in all incident energy being transmitted. This is all the more surprising since CFT
2
is in the confined phase, and one could have expected that fewer degrees of freedom are available to conduct heat. The microscopic mechanism behind this surprising phenomenon deserves to be studied further.

The above discussion stays valid for finite temperature difference
\Theta_{+}-\Theta_{-}
, but the dominant phase cannot in this case be found by comparing free energies. Nevertheless, as
\Delta x\to 0
we expect from the dual ICFT that the interface-antiinterface pair fuses into the trivial (identity) defect
14
14

14

In some range of parameters (
\lambda\geq\lambda_{0}
,
\ell_{2}<3\ell_{1}
), the pair fuses into a non-trivial defect
[
189
]
, although this configuration is at best metastable.

[
141
]
, whereas at very large
\Delta x
the connected solution ceases
to exist. A transition is therefore bound to occur between these extreme separations.

Let us comment finally on what happens if the interval theory is CFT
1
, the theory with fewer degrees of freedom, and the outside theory is CFT
2
. Here the low-temperature phase only exists for sufficiently-large tension if
c_{1}<c_{2}<3c_{1}
, and does not exist if
c_{2}>3c_{1}

[
189
,
77
]
. The (sparse) degrees of freedom of the interval theory in this latter case are always in the high-temperature phase, and there can be no quantum-coherent conduction of heat. Reassuringly, this includes the limit
c_{2}/c_{1}\to 0
in which the CFT
1
interval is effectively void.

Note also that in the low-temperature phase the wire can be compactified to a circle and the heat current can be sustained without external reservoirs. This is not possible in the high-temperature phase.

3.8
Closing remarks

The study of far-from-equilibrium quantum systems is an exciting frontier both in condensed-matter physics and in quantum gravity. Holography is a bridge between these two areas of research and has led to many new insights. Much remains however to be understood, and simple tractable models can help as testing grounds for new ideas. The holographic NESS of this paper are tractable thanks to several simplifying factors: 2d conformal symmetry, isolated impurities and the assumption of a thin brane. If the first two can be justified in (very) pure ballistic systems, the thin-brane approximation is an ad-hoc assumption of convenience. Extending these results to top-down dual pairs is an important step to validate them.

Another obvious question concerns the structure of entanglement and the Hubeny-Rangamani-Ryu-Takayanagi curves
[
86
,
96
]
in the above steady states. While it is known that geodesics cannot probe the region behind equilibrium horizons
[
199
]
, they can reach behind both apparent and event horizons in time-dependent backgrounds, see e.g.
[
200
,
201
,
129
]
. In the framework of the fluid/gravity correspondence the entropy current associated with the event horizon is a local functional of the boundary data
[
202
]
. It would be interesting to examine this question in the present far-from-equilibrium context. In the next chapter, we provide some partial results in this direction.

Another interesting question is how the deconfinement transition of the interval CFT in section
3.7
relates to the sudden jump in thermal conductivity of the system. Last but not least, it would be nice to relate the production of entropy to the scattering matrix of microscopic interfaces, e.g. for the simplest free-field interfaces of
[
18
,
141
,
154
]
. Presumably, this is also a computation that could be completed holographically, with a slightly more complicated model.

Chapter 4
Entanglement entropy and holographic interfaces

Based on unpublished work

This final chapter is dedicated to the study of the entanglement entropy structure in the models that were constructed in the previous two chapters. There are several motivations for why this is an interesting quantity to study.

First and foremost, it is in direct connection with the doubly holographic black hole evaporation models quickly described in sec.
1.9
. Thus if one wants to use this holographic model to test the Island formula, it is necessary to be able to compute RT surfaces. Indeed, this is what was studied in
[
20
]
.

On a related note, the study of RT surfaces in such spacetimes is interesting in and of themselves because there generally are several competing RT surfaces. As we change the boundary interval, there will be discontinuous jumps in the RT surfaces, a sort of phase transition in the entanglement structure. In the context of the doubly-holographic models and the page curve, this is related to the Page time as is explained in sec.
1.9
.

From the point of view of the ICFT, such transitions might be related to some other property of the underlying state. For instance, one of our conjectures was that the presence (or not) of a center in one side of the ICFT dual (see
2.7.2
) might be related to the crossing (or not) of the RT surface computing the Von Neumann entropy of associated CFT. This turned out to be incorrect, but the connections of RT surfaces with bulk reconstruction
[
104
]
suggests there should be a way to relate the sweeping transition with the entanglement structure of the ICFT.

Another interesting direction was alluded in chap.
3
, and it is the computation of RT surfaces in non-equilibrium solutions with deformed horizons. Indeed, it is still an open question how is the Bekenstein-Hawking formula modified whenever we consider out-of-equilibrium horizons, which are non-Killing. Is it the event horizon or the apparent horizon that must appear in the formula
[
203
]
? In equilibrium situations, those two coincide, so the distinction cannot be made. Additionally, this may also allow us to understand and confirm the entropy production at the interface of the NESS state described in the previous chapter.

Finally, entanglement entropy computations allow one to compute the
Q
uantum
N
ull
E
nergy
C
ondition (QNEC)
[
204
]
. As we will explain in the main text, the QNEC is an inequality (which should hold in any QFT) relating derivatives of the entanglement entropy and the one-point function of the Stress-Energy tensor. As such, it is a powerful tool to determine if simple holographic bottom-up models are at least consistent. For instance, the QNEC has been used to restrict quenches of CFTs
[
205
,
206
,
207
]
, by using the RT prescription in the dual bulk. Such a quench geometry is obtained in much the same way as the models of chap.
2
, the difference being the membrane is now spacelike, instead of timelike. Whenever the QNEC is not obeyed in such a quench, it signals that it is probably unphysical. In the same vein, it would be interesting to compute the QNEC in the various geometries that were obtained in the previous chapters to check for consistency.

Since this chapter is composed of partial, incomplete and sometimes inconclusive results, it will necessarily seem much more scattered and less polished than the previous ones. While there isn’t a clear-cut goal or thread, but rather several different directions, we believe that the work we present here is valuable, and hope that it will be completed and ”packaged” in the more standard form of a paper sometime.

Throughout this chapter, we use units in which
8\pi G=1
.

4.1
Geodesics in asymptotically Anti-de-Sitter spaces

The main object of study in this chapter are RT and HRT surfaces in 3-dimensional asymptotically AdS spacetimes. In fact, we can restrict to the study of spacetimes which are locally AdS, since the only matter content of our models is the membrane, and everywhere else the vacuum equations of motion hold. In three dimensions, finding RT curves amounts to computing spatial geodesics anchored at the asymptotic boundary.

In this section, we compute and outline some properties of such geodesics, mainly in Poincaré space. Indeed, we solved the equations in one coordinate system, by exploiting diffeomorphisms between the different geometries, we can obtain the geodesics in any locally AdS spacetime.

Let us begin by recalling the Poincaré metric :

\displaystyle ds^{2}=\frac{\ell^{2}}{z^{2}}(dw_{+}dw_{-}+dz^{2})\ ,

(4.1)

where
w_{-}=x-t
,
w_{+}=x+t
are the lightcone coordinates. Consider the most generic spacelike geodesic, of initial point
p_{0}=(w_{+}^{0},w_{+}^{0},z_{0})
and tangent vector
\dot{p}_{0}=(\dot{w}_{+}^{0},\dot{w}_{-}^{0},\dot{z}_{0})
. We will consider WLOG affinely parametrized geodesics, namely :

\displaystyle\dot{p}^{\mu}\dot{p}_{\mu}=\frac{\ell^{2}}{z^{2}}\left(\dot{w}_{+}\dot{w}_{-}+\dot{z}^{2}\right)=1\ ,

(4.2)

where this condition will hold not only at the initial point, but everywhere along the geodesic, so we omit the ”0” indices.

Denoting the affine parameter
\lambda
, the action that we need to minimize is simply :

\displaystyle L=\int\frac{\ell^{2}}{z^{2}}(\dot{w}_{+}\dot{w}_{-}+\dot{z}^{2})d\lambda\ .

(4.3)

Using cyclicity of
w_{+}
,
w_{-}
we get two integration constants :

K_{\pm}=\frac{\dot{w}_{\pm}\ell}{z^{2}}\ .

(4.4)

Translations of the affine parameter give us the conservation of the tangent vector norm (
4.2
) :

\displaystyle K_{-}K_{+}z^{2}+\frac{\ell^{2}\dot{z}^{2}}{z^{2}}=1\ .

(4.5)

Note that other symmetries like scale invariance (
z\rightarrow\omega z
,
w_{\pm}\rightarrow\omega w_{\pm}
) or boosts (
w_{+}\rightarrow\gamma w_{+},w_{-}\rightarrow\gamma^{-1}w_{-}
) do not provide additional integration constants.

We can solve (
4.5
) for
z(\lambda)
. There are three cases which we must differentiate,
K_{-}K_{+}>0
,
K_{+}K_{-}<0
and
K_{+}K_{-}=0
. These signs are simply determined by the initial vectors, since
{\rm sign}(K_{-}K_{+})={\rm sign}(\dot{w}_{-}\dot{w}_{+})
. Thus, in Poincaré coordinates, we will have three types of qualitatively different geodesics.

4.1.1
Case
K_{-}K_{+}>0

We begin by the
K_{-}K_{+}>0
case. Upon integrating (
4.5
) :

\displaystyle\pm(\frac{\lambda}{\ell}+C)={\rm Arctanh}\left(\sqrt{1-K_{+}K_{-}z^{2}}\right)\Leftrightarrow z(\lambda)=\sqrt{\frac{1-\tanh^{2}(\lambda/\ell+C)}{K_{+}K_{-}}}\ .

(4.6)

The
\pm
signs denotes two branches of the solution which join at
z=\frac{1}{K_{+}K_{-}}
, for
\lambda=-C\ell
. The
C
integration constant can be absorbed into a translation of the affine parameter, we set
C=0
so that the ”turning point” of the geodesic is always at
\lambda=0
. Then in (
4.6
) the plus sign must be chosen for
\lambda>0
and minus sign for
\lambda<0
. For this choice, the initial
\lambda_{0}
is given as :

\lambda_{0}=-\ell\;{\rm sign}(\dot{z}_{0}){\rm Arctanh}\left(\sqrt{1-\frac{\ell^{2}}{z_{0}^{2}}\dot{w}_{+}^{0}\dot{w}_{-}^{0}}\right)\ .

(4.7)

Plugging (
4.6
) into (
4.4
) and performing the integration yields :

w_{\pm}=\frac{1}{K_{\mp}}\tanh(\lam/\ell)+G_{\pm}\ ,

(4.8)

where
G_{\pm}
are two integration constants. They are easily fixed using the initial conditions, and the full solution is given by :

\displaystyle z(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\frac{z_{0}^{2}}{\ell}\sqrt{\frac{1-\tanh^{2}(\lambda/\ell)}{\dot{w}_{+}^{0}\dot{w}_{-}^{0}}}\Leftrightarrow\lambda=\pm\ell\sqrt{1-\frac{z(\lambda)^{2}}{z_{0}^{2}}\frac{\ell^{2}}{z_{0}^{2}}\dot{w}_{+}^{0}\dot{w}_{-}^{0}}\ ,

(4.9)

\displaystyle w_{\pm}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\frac{\tanh(\lam/\ell)-\tanh(\lam_0/\ell)}{\ell\dot{w}_{\mp}^{0}}z_{0}^{2}+w_{\pm}^{0}\ ,

\displaystyle\lambda_{0}

\displaystyle=-\ell\,{\rm sign}(\dot{z}_{0}){\rm Arctanh}\left(\sqrt{1-\frac{\ell^{2}}{z_{0}^{2}}\dot{w}_{+}^{0}\dot{w}_{-}^{0}}\right)\ .

These are the geodesics one usually thinks of in AdS space. At
\lambda=\pm\infty
, they are anchored on the boundary of AdS. In the case where
\dot{w}^{0}_{+}=\dot{w}^{0}_{-}
, they are semi-circles centered on the asymptotic boundary. By boosting them one can obtain the general shape of the geodesic.

Note that while it would be nice to parametrize the geodesic by its initial point on the asymptotic boundary, this can’t be done as they lie formally at infinity. Indeed, as we send
z_{0}=\epsilon\rightarrow 0
, to satisfy the affine condition we need
\dot{w}^{0}_{\pm}\propto\epsilon^{2}
and
\dot{z}_{0}\propto\epsilon
, so on the boundary the tangent vector is degenerate.

4.1.2
Case
K_{-}K_{+}<0

We treat the case
K_{-}K_{+}<0
. The resolution of the equations is essentially the same, modulo the change
{\rm tanh}\rightarrow{\rm coth}
. Skipping the details, we have :

\displaystyle z(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\frac{z_{0}^{2}}{\ell}\sqrt{\frac{1-\coth^{2}(\lambda/\ell)}{\dot{w}^{0}_{+}\dot{w}^{0}_{-}}}\ ,

(4.10)

\displaystyle w_{\pm}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\frac{\coth(\lambda/\ell)-\coth(\lambda_{0}/\ell)}{\ell\dot{w}^{0}_{\mp}}z_{0}^{2}+w^{0}_{\pm}\ ,

\displaystyle\lambda_{0}

\displaystyle=-\ell\,{\rm sign}(\dot{z}_{0}){\rm Arccoth}\left(\sqrt{1-\frac{\ell^{2}}{z_{0}^{2}}\dot{w}^{0}_{+}\dot{w}^{0}_{-}}\right)\ .

Note that now, the point
\lambda=0
is not really a ”turning point” anymore since as we approach it all the coordinates diverge as
\frac{1}{\lambda}
. For the two signs of
\lambda
, we get two disconnected solutions, with one end in the Poincaré horizon
z=\infty
and the other on the boundary
z=0
. Naturally, the change
\lambda\rightarrow-\lambda
is equivalent to a reversal of the initial tangent vector
\dot{w}_{\pm}^{0}\rightarrow-\dot{w}_{\pm}^{0}
, along with a translation of the initial point of
-\frac{2\coth(\lambda_{0}/\ell)z_{0}^{2}}{\ell\dot{w}_{\mp}^{0}}
. See fig.
4.1
for a sketch of the geodesic projected to the
t=cst
plane (note that there are no geodesics of this type that stay in this plane, because
\dot{t}_{0}\neq 0
necessarily).

Figure 4.1
:
Plot of the two disconnected branches of the geodesic of equation (
4.10
). Both branches escape out to the Poincaré horizon. If we maximally extend, we would find that they end up hitting the boundary again, as all spacelike geodesics have two anchor points on the boundary in global coordinates.

These geodesic seem very strange, but this is due to the coordinate system we chose. Indeed, the Poincaré horizon is a coordinate singularity, and if we were to map these geodesics to global coordinates, we would see that they are a portion of a geodesic that is doubly anchored at the boundary. Nonetheless, for the purposes of the RT prescription, the coordinate system in which we look for the geodesics will of course matter, since it will depend on the state of the CFT.

4.1.3
Case
K_{-}K_{+}=0

The last case to consider is of ”measure zero” in the set of initial conditions, the case
K_{-}K_{+}=0
. It includes in particular geodesics that are shot ”straight” toward the bulk interior.

Solving (
4.5
) in this case gives us

z=e^{{\rm sign}{\dot{z}_{0}}\lambda/\ell}\ ,

(4.11)

where we absorbed the integration constant into a translation of
\lambda
. The equation (
4.4
) is again readily solved, regrouping all the equations we obtain :

\displaystyle z

\displaystyle={\rm exp}\left({\rm sign}(\dot{z}_{0})\frac{\lambda}{\ell}\right)\ ,

(4.12)

\displaystyle w_{\pm}

\displaystyle=\frac{\dot{w}^{0}_{\pm}\ell}{2z_{0}^{2}}(e^{2{\rm sign}(\dot{z}_{0})\lambda/\ell}-e^{2\lambda_{0}/\ell})+w^{0}_{\pm}\ ,

\displaystyle\lambda_{0}

\displaystyle=\ell\;{\rm sign}(\dot{z}_{0})\ln(z_0)\ .

Of course, in (
4.12
), one of the
\dot{w}^{0}_{\pm}
must be equal to zero if the equations are to be satisfied.

4.2
Simple examples

Let us apply the formulas we have derived above to some standard examples. Consider an interval of length
a
on the boundary of Poincaré space. The dual CFT state is simply the vacuum on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, so computing the entanglement entropy of the interval should reproduce the classic result by Calabrese and Cardy
[
85
]
.

Consider the initial point to be
(t=0,x=0,z=\epsilon)
on the boundary, where we introduced the IR cutoff
\epsilon
. Since the geodesics exhibited in the previous section are parametrized in terms of the initial tangent vector
\dot{p}^{0}
and not the final point, we must invert some relations to find the correct values for which the geodesic ends on
(t=0,x=a,z=0)
1
1

1

In poincaré space, it is possible to invert the relation generally and to give the geodesics as parametrized by initial and final points. We don’t find it useful to reproduce it here, as it is more cumbersome than the parametrization we exhibited and, while it can be convenient, we won’t really need it for our purposes.
.

We already know
\dot{w}_{+}\dot{w}_{-}>0
in order to have a geodesic connecting two points on the boundary. The endpoint is located at
z=\epsilon
, at
\lambda=-\lambda_{0}
. From the equation of
w_{\pm}(-\lambda_{0})
(
4.9
), we find :

\displaystyle\dot{w}^{0}_{+}=\dot{w}^{0}_{-}\equiv\dot{w}_{0}=\frac{\epsilon}{\ell\sqrt{\frac{a^{2}}{4\epsilon^{2}}+1}}\ .

(4.13)

What remains is to compute the length of the geodesic. Thankfully, in the affine parametrization this is simply given by the difference in initial and final affine parameters:

\displaystyle L=-2\lambda_{0}=2\ell{\rm Arctanh}\left(\frac{\dot{w}_{0}\ell a}{2\epsilon^{2}}\right)=2\ell{\rm Arctanh}\left(\frac{a}{\sqrt{a^{2}+4\epsilon^{2}}}\right)\ .

(4.14)

To obtain the entanglement entropy, all we have to do is divide by
4G=\frac{1}{2\pi}
and take the limit
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
. We obtain at leading order :

\displaystyle S=2\pi L=4\pi\ell\ln\left(\frac{a}{\epsilon}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{2})\right)=\frac{c}{3}\ln\left(\frac{a}{\epsilon}\right)+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{2})\ ,

(4.15)

which correctly reproduces the expected result
[
85
]
.

Let us illustrate a slightly more complicated example, which will be crucial for the methods of computation we will use in the ICFT setups. We consider now the spinning string geometry, the metric (
3.1
). In this case, spacetime is only stationary, but not static. The problem thus becomes immediately more complex; instead of studying curves restricted to a 2-dimensional Cauchy slice, we must allow for generic spatial geodesic in the full 3D geometry. Furthermore, one can try to solve the geodesic equations for the metric (
3.1
), and it is feasible but a much harder problem.

Instead, we exploit the change of coordinate to bring us back to the Poincaré geometry. To find the diffeomorphism relating the two coordinate systems one can for instance equate the two parametrizations of the AdS hyperboloid described in sec.
1.1
, and solve the resulting equations. We find (
\sigma={\rm sign}(J)
):

\displaystyle w_{+}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{r^{2}-r_{+}^{2}}{r^{2}-r_{-}^{2}}}\exp\left(\frac{(r_{+}-\sigma r_{-})(x+t)}{\ell}\right)\ ,

(4.16)

\displaystyle w_{-}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{r^{2}-r_{+}^{2}}{r^{2}-r_{-}^{2}}}\exp\left(\frac{(r_{+}+\sigma r_{-})(x-t)}{\ell}\right)\ ,

\displaystyle z

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{r_{+}^{2}-r_{-}^{2}}{r^{2}-r_{-}^{2}}}\exp\left(\frac{xr_{+}-\sigma tr_{-}}{\ell}\right)\ .

Of course, (
4.16
) is far from unique, as we can apply any isometry of Poincaré and still obtain a valid change of coordinates. Note in passing that this coordinate change covers only a portion of Poincaré space, since
w_{+}>0\cap w_{-}>0
, and only a portion of the spinning string, as the coordinate change becomes singular at the horizon
r=r_{+}
2
2

2

The outer horizon is mapped to
w_{-}=0\cup w_{+}=0
. A more careful analysis would show that this surface is composed in fact of two horizons, the future, and past ones. If one extends to the rest of the Poincaré spacetime, we would obtain the two interior regions, as well as another exterior.
. There are other similar changes of coordinates that map the region
r_{-}<r<r_{+}
and
0<r<r_{-}
, but we won’t be needing them here.

Consider now the boundary region in stationary CFT delimited by two points
(t=0,r=1/\epsilon,x=0)
and
(t=0,r=1/\epsilon,x=a)
. WLOG we take
\sigma=1
, namely
J>0
. Under the change of coordinates (
4.16
), these are mapped to

\displaystyle p_{1}=

\displaystyle\left(w_{+}=1,w_{-}=1,z=\epsilon\sqrt{r_{+}^{2}-r_{-}^{2}}\right)\ ,

(4.17)

\displaystyle p_{2}=

\displaystyle\left(w_{+}=\exp(\frac{r_+-r_-}{\ell}a),w_{-}=\exp(\frac{r_++r_-}{\ell}a),z=\epsilon\sqrt{r_{+}^{2}-r_{-}^{2}}\exp(\frac{xr_+}{\ell})\right)\ ,

where we kept only the leading order in
\epsilon
. We will write
p_{i}^{b}
for the corresponding points on the 2D-boundary, with the
z
-coordinate excised.

Note that we do not care about the specific shape of the boundary interval we select between the two points. Indeed, any spacelike curve between
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
will have the same entanglement entropy, because they all lie in the same ”causal diamond”
3
3

3

The causal diamond of a portion of a Cauchy slice
A
is the region that is in causal contact uniquely with
A
. For instance, if
A
is the interval between two points
x
,
y
, the causal diamond is determined by shooting lightrays from
x
and
y
, and selecting the enclosed region. Because points in this region can only be affected by
A
, they can be expressed as a unitary transformation applied to
A
. See fig.
4.13
.
. Any two of these curve houses states which are related by a unitary transformation, and thus have the same entanglement entropy, see sec.
1.8
.

We can exploit this fact to simplify the problem even further. Indeed using the points (
4.17
), we would still need to apply the HRT prescription, as they do not lie on a preferred Cauchy slice. Then, we use the isometries of Poincaré to bring these two points on a constant time slice. In this case, we can begin by a
x
-translation to bring
p_{1}
to the origin. Then, we perform a boost to bring
p_{2}
on the same
t=0
Cauchy slice. Since these are isometries of Minkowski on the boundary, we do not need to perform them explicitly, as we now that the norm
|p^{b}_{1}-p^{b}_{2}|
will be conserved. We find :

\displaystyle|p_{2}^{b}-p_{1}^{b}|^{2}=4\exp(\frac{r_+ a}{\ell}){\rm sinh}\left(\frac{r_{+}-r_{-}}{2\ell}a\right){\rm sinh}\left(\frac{r_{+}+r_{-}}{2\ell}a\right)\equiv\tilde{a}\ .

(4.18)

In this way, we successfully reduced the problem to the same RT computation as previously with an interval of size
\tilde{a}
. There is one difference, and that is in the UV cutoffs which are now different at the two endpoints. Recycling (
4.13
) and computing the correct
\lambda_{f}
given the different cutoffs, we obtain :

\displaystyle S=2\pi L=2\pi(\lambda_{f}-\lambda_{0})=\frac{c}{6}\log(\frac{ 4}{\eps^2 (r_+^2-r_-^2)}{\rm sinh}\left(\frac{r_+-r_-}{2\ell}a\right){\rm sinh}\left(\frac{r_++r_-}{2\ell}a\right))\ ,

(4.19)

which correctly recovers the expected entanglement entropy, see (
3.10
).

With this method, inverting (
4.16
), it is also possible to obtain the equation of the HRT surface in the original coordinate system. In particular, the spacelike geodesic that we obtain does not remain in a constant time plane, as we should expect for a non-static geometry. A sketch of the HRT surface, projected on the
t=0
plane, can be found on fig.
4.2
. Notice that the HRT surface cannot breach the horizon, and as
a\rightarrow\infty
, it will approach it more and more, which gives the Bekenstein-Hawking formula for the coarse-grained entropy (see (
3.11
)).

Figure 4.2
:
Sketch of an HRT surface between two equal time points on the boundary,
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
. The HRT surface is not confined to the constant time Cauchy slice depicted here, rather it is its projection onto this plane. We can see that the HRT surface drops very quickly close to the horizon, where it can reduce its length. Spacelike geodesics are not forbidden from entering the horizons, but there are no spacelike geodesics entering the horizon with two anchor points on the same boundary, see app.
D.3

4.3
Entanglement structure of static ICFT states

In this section, we apply the lessons we have just learned to compute the entanglement structure of static ICFT states on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. We will also mention possible applications to the compact geometries of chap.
2
.

4.3.1
RT surfaces with interfaces

In this section, we wish to apply the RT prescription to spacetimes containing a gravitating domain wall. As the wall connects two separate bulks, we need a prescription to continue the RT surface across the wall. For instance, in the case of end-of-the-world branes which appear in Island computations
[
21
,
22
]
, the prescription states that the RT surface should end perpendicularly on the End of the World brane
4
4

4

This is only in the case where the EOW brane is described by the simplest Lagrangian, only involving its tension. If additional fields are living on it, the prescription will change
. We will show that this can be recovered this as a special limit of the more general interface prescription.

Consider again the action determining a spacelike geodesic, but on a spacetime which is composed of two pieces. Each piece will contain a domain wall which we parametrise as
x^{\mu}_{mi}(\xi^{a})
, where the
i
denotes its embedding on side
i
. The geodesic is parametrised by
\lambda
, and crosses the membrane at
\lambda^{*}
:

\displaystyle L=\int_{\lambda_{0}}^{\lambda^{*}}\sqrt{g^{1}_{\mu\nu}\dot{x}^{\mu}\dot{x}^{\nu}}d\lambda+\int_{\lambda^{*}}^{\lambda_{0}}\sqrt{g^{2}_{\mu\nu}\dot{x}^{\mu}\dot{x}^{\nu}}d\lambda=\int_{\lambda_{0}}^{\lambda^{*}}\mathcal{L}_{1}(x,\dot{x})d\lambda+\int_{\lambda^{*}}^{\lambda_{0}}\mathcal{L}_{2}(x,\dot{x})d\lambda\ .

(4.20)

In what follows, we omit the second half of the action as it is treated similarly. In the variation of (
4.20
), the initial and final points are fixed,
\delta x(\lambda_{0})=\delta x(\lambda_{f})=0
. On the other hand, the variation at
\lambda^{*}
receives a softer constraint; it must simply remain on the membrane. Using the parametrization of the membrane, we can write is as :

\displaystyle\delta x^{\rho}(\lambda^{*})=\frac{\partial x^{\rho}_{m}}{\partial\xi^{a}}\delta\xi^{a}\ ,

(4.21)

where the
\delta\xi^{a}
are arbitrary variations. The variation of (
4.20
) then reads :

\displaystyle\delta L=\int_{\lambda_{0}}^{\lambda^{*}}\frac{\delta\mathcal{L}_{1}}{\delta x^{\sigma}(\lambda)}\delta x^{\sigma}(\lambda)+\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}_{1}}{\partial\dot{x}^{\rho}}\delta x^{\rho}(\lambda^{*})+1\leftrightarrow 2\ ,

(4.22)

where we used the shorthand
\frac{\delta\mathcal{L}_{1}}{\delta x^{\sigma}(\lambda)}=\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}_{1}}{\partial x^{\sigma}}-\frac{d}{d\lambda}\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}_{1}}{\partial\dot{x}^{\sigma}}
, which is the term which will give the Euler-Lagrange equations. The second term in (
4.22
) appears from an integration by parts.

Thus by the arbitrariness of the variation
\delta x^{\sigma}(\lambda)
, we obtain the geodesic equations which must be obeyed by the geodesic on both sides. The ”crossing constraints” are obtained by requiring that the boundary term vanishes. They are :

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}_{1}}{\partial\dot{x}^{\rho}}\frac{\partial x^{\rho}_{m}}{\partial\xi^{a}}=\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}_{2}}{\partial\dot{x}^{\rho}}\frac{\partial x^{\rho}_{m}}{\partial\xi^{a}}\ ,

(4.23)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\frac{t^{1\,\rho}_{a}\dot{x}^{\sigma}_{1}g_{1\,\rho\sigma}}{\sqrt{g_{1\mu\nu}\dot{x}_{1}^{\mu}\dot{x}_{1}^{\nu}}}=\frac{t^{2\,\rho}_{a}\dot{x}^{\sigma}_{2}g_{2\,\rho\sigma}}{\sqrt{g_{2\mu\nu}\dot{x}_{2}^{\mu}\dot{x}_{2}^{\nu}}}\ ,

where we labeled by
i
the curves lying on side
i
. We introduced the notation
t^{i\,\rho}_{a}=\frac{\partial x^{\rho}_{m}}{\partial\xi^{a}}
which are the tangent vectors to the membrane.

The constraints (
4.23
) tell us that the projection of membrane tangent vectors on the geodesic should be conserved while crossing.

One can get a bit more intuition on the meaning of this constraint by considering the membrane to contain only spacelike directions. In this case, the tangent vectors
t^{i\,\mu}_{a}
are all spacelike, and using the metric matching condition (
2.4
), we have that the scalar products
t^{i\,\mu}_{a}t^{i\,\nu}_{b}g_{i\mu\nu}=h_{ab}
are equal on both sides, since they are the membrane’s induced metric. This allows us to divide on both sides of (
4.23
) by the norm of the tangent vectors. The resulting equations then equate the angles of the geodesic with the membrane :

\displaystyle\sphericalangle\left(t^{1}_{a},\dot{x}_{1}\right)=\sphericalangle\left(t^{2}_{a},\dot{x}_{2}\right)\ .

(4.24)

Note that the condition (
4.23
) can only be interpreted as (
4.24
) in the case where the metric matching is satisfied. Indeed, there is an intuitive way of understanding why is this the case. The metric matching condition is essentially a continuity equation on the metric. This should imply that the Christoffel symbols are at most step-wise discontinuous. Then by the geodesic equation :

\displaystyle\ddot{x}^{\mu}=\Gamma^{\mu}_{\nu\rho}\dot{x}^{\nu}\dot{x}^{\rho}\ .

(4.25)

By integrating we find that
\dot{x}^{\mu}
should be continuous across the interface. The precise way this is realized is simply (
4.24
).

In the case where some directions of the membrane are timelike, the norm squared of the tangent vectors will be negative. We could still divide on both sides by the absolute value of the norm, but the expression that we obtain in this way is difficult to visualize as an ”angle”.

4.3.2
Vacuum ICFT

We are now ready to compute RT surfaces in setups containing a gravitating wall. We consider in this section the joining of two CFTs in their vacuum state, on an infinite interval. The solution is described in sec.
2.3.2
, we rewrite here the crucial formulas to set slightly different conventions. The dual metrics can be given in Poincaré coordinates :

\displaystyle ds^{2}_{j}=\frac{\ell_{j}^{2}}{z_{j}^{2}}\left(-dt^{2}+dx_{j}^{2}+dz_{j}^{2})\right)\ ,

(4.26)

and the membrane equation is very simple in these coordinates :

\displaystyle x_{j}(z_{j})=-\frac{\tan(\psi_j)}{z_{j}}\ ,

(4.27)

where the solution is given in the folded picture, and the conventions are to keep the part of spacetime that lies to the ”increasing x” side of the membrane (for convenience we inverted here the convention with respect to previous chapters). The identification is done by setting
\ell_{1}/z_{1}=\ell_{2}/z_{2}\Leftrightarrow\cos(\psi_1)/z_{1}=\cos(\psi_2)/z_{2}
along the membrane.

Figure 4.3
:
Example of a glued geometry dual to a vacuum ICFT state. The depicted angles
\psi_{i}
have positive values in the picture. The arrows denote that the two membranes should be identified.

Remember that in the minimal model, the only parameters available are the AdS radii
\ell_{i}
and the tension
\lambda
of the membrane, as here, the state is fixed to the vacuum. From the gluing equations (
2.27
), we can trade
\ell_{2}/\ell_{1}
and
\lambda
for the angles
\psi_{i}
. The WLOG assumptions
\ell_{1}\leq\ell_{2}
as well as the Israel equations then impose :

\displaystyle 0<\psi_{1}\leq\pi/2\;\;;-\psi_{1}<\psi_{2}<\psi_{1}\ .

(4.28)

So, for any two angles in the range (
4.28
), we have an ICFT dual for definite
\frac{c_{2}}{c_{1}}
and membrane tension
\lambda
.

The simplicity of the solution in this case allows us to explicitly construct a coordinate system unifying the two spacetime pieces
[
50
]
. Then, the full solution can be expressed in a single coordinate chart, see fig.
4.4
.

Figure 4.4
:
The same solutions depicted in fig.
4.3
, but presented in an unified coordinate chart. One can verify that the way we glued the two spacetimes, they respect the gluing condition
cos(\psi_{1})/z_{1}=\cos(\psi_2)/z_{2}
.

We will see that in this picture, crossing RT surfaces can be expressed by a beautiful geometric construction, which is due to
[
183
]
