) even though such configurations sometimes appeared naturally in cases where the membrane intersected itself. While they should not alter the conclusions on the phase space drastically, this more complete model could exhibit some interesting properties of the pair of interfaces.

A related question concerns the ”exotic fusion” geometries in which, as we bring the two interfaces together, they do not fuse into the trivial defect (fig.
2.10
). Their mere existence is quite surprising
[
222
,
223
]
, and it might be related to the ”membrane fusion” geometries mentioned above. This is because deeper regions of the AdS bulk are supposed to probe the IR region of the dual field theory. As we go to the IR the two interfaces approach one another and eventually appear as one, ”fusing”. Thus, an allowable non-trivial membrane fusion in the bulk might signal the presence of a potential exotic interface fusion in the field theory.

One last problem that deserves future work is the elucidation of the meaning of the sweeping transition (sec.
2.7.2
) from the boundary theory. Our strong feeling is that they must be connected to the entanglement structure of the field theory, as it is the quantity that is related to the bulk reconstruction. We have arrived at some conjectures in chap.
4
, but they are still very tentative. Related to this problem is the role of the critical tension
\lambda_{0}
. It is still unclear if this is simply a quantity that appears in the computations, or whether it carries a deeper meaning.

In chap.
3
we looked at an extension of the static states of chap.
2
to more general non-equilibrium stationary states. This is of interest both to confirm the work that was already done from the field theory perspective
[
70
,
133
,
51
]
and extend it, but also to generate tractable non-equilibrium black hole solutions, which are still poorly understood. These goals were met. First, our calculation confirmed the universality of the energy-transport coefficients shown in ICFT in
[
51
]
. Secondly, it offers a much simpler way to compute these coefficients on the gravity side, when compared to the original scattering calculations of
[
153
]
. Lastly, perhaps the least expected result was the discovery of the maximal entropy production at the interface and the analytical recovery of the deformed, non-killing event horizon on the gravity side.

These results naturally lead to the fascinating question of the relation of the deformed horizon with entanglement entropy. We touched on this issue in chap.
4
and hope to complete our partial results in the near future. The well-known Bekenstein-Hawking formula, which has been re-derived with the RT prescription in AdS/CFT, has been mainly applied to black holes with static killing horizons. In this model, we have an example of an (analytically controlled) event horizon that is neither Killing, nor coinciding with its ”apparent” counterpart. It is the perfect setup to elucidate the role played by these various horizons in holography.

One other question that needs elucidation concerns the thermal transmission of the interface pairs. For a single interface, we recovered results previously obtained perturbatively
[
24
]
, but in the case of more than one interface, there appeared two distinct phases. When the membrane avoids the black hole and curves back to the boundary, the conductance suddenly becomes perfect. This behavior certainly pertains to some sort of coherent scattering, which allows all the thermal flux through as if there were no interfaces. The precise way in which this happens in the field theory is however unclear and deserves further study.

The final chapter
4
ties the project together as it brings us back to the initial goal of connecting our models to the Island computations. We have already mentioned that the computations of RT surfaces in the obtained geometries was the obvious next step in their study, and that is what is undertaken in this chapter. Based on the vacuum computations of
[
20
]
which we present, we outline different angles (analytical and numerical) to tackle computations of the sought-out RT surfaces.

While some interesting results were obtained, we haven’t yet managed to reach the initial goal: showing the existence of horizon probing RT surfaces in the non-equilibrium case, and understanding the entropy production at the interface. The main focus of future work will be the completion of these goals. Finally, another tangent direction that was mentioned is the computation of the QNEC bound for these same geometries. Its application to the ICFT model will cement its consistency, while potentially providing interesting insights on the necessary conditions for its saturation. The QNEC becomes much more interesting when one considers the Wick-rotated version of our models
[
77
,
205
]
, in which the interface now plays the role of a quench. It could be interesting to see which restrictions it imposes if any, and whether it tells us something about the non Wick-rotated version.

Although much was accomplished, much still remains to be worked out, as is always the case in physics.

Appendix A
Misc

A.1
Extrinsic curvature

Consider a surface of codimension one embedded in a manifold of dimension
D
. It is parametrized by (D-1) parameters denoted
s^{a}
. In a Lorentzian manifold, one should differentiate according to the nature of the hypersurface (lightlike, spacelike or timelike). Unless otherwise stated, we assume here that the surface is timelike. Generally, its equation can be written as

\displaystyle x^{\mu}=x^{\mu}(s^{a})\ ,

(A.1)

where
x^{\mu}(s^{a})
are
D
functions specifying the surface’s shape. We can then naturally define a set of
D-1
tangent vectors to the surface :

\displaystyle t_{a}^{\mu}=\frac{\partial x^{\mu}}{\partial s^{a}}\ ,

(A.2)

which allows us to define the induced metric :

\displaystyle h_{ab}=g_{\mu\nu}t_{a}^{\mu}t_{b}^{\nu}\ ,

(A.3)

where
g_{\mu\nu}
is the metric of the ambient spacetime. To define the normal covector
n_{\mu}
, one can use the generalized cross-product :

\displaystyle n_{\mu}=\frac{1}{2}\epsilon_{\mu\nu_{1}\ldots\nu_{D-1}}\epsilon^{a_{1}\ldots a_{D-1}}t^{\nu_{1}}_{a_{1}}\ldots t^{\nu_{D-1}}_{a_{D-1}}\ ,

(A.4)

where the
\epsilon
symbols are the levi-civita tensors corresponding to the metric
g
and
h
(with the appropriate normalisation with
\sqrt{|g|}
). Using (
A.4
), the normal vector is normalized,
n^{\mu}n_{\mu}=1
. (For a lightlike surface,
n^{\mu}n_{\mu}=0
, and for a spacelike surface,
n^{\mu}n_{\mu}=-1
).

In the case where the surface can be defined in terms of an equation
f(x^{\mu})=0
, there is a much simpler way to obtain the normal co-vector :

\displaystyle n_{\mu}=\frac{\partial_{\nu}f}{\sqrt{\partial_{\nu}f\partial^{\nu}f}}\ ,

(A.5)

which is obtained by varying the defining equation along tangent directions. The normal vector of course satisfies :

\displaystyle n_{\mu}t^{\mu}_{a}=0\ .

(A.6)

Using it, we can define the projection operator on the surface :

\displaystyle\Pi^{\mu}_{\nu}=\delta^{\mu}_{\nu}-n_{\nu}n^{\mu}

(A.7)

Note that formally,
n_{\mu}
and hence
\Pi^{\mu}_{\nu}
are defined only on the surface
x^{\mu}(s^{a})
. Thus they are not vector fields on the ambient manifold. One can however arbitrarily extend them to all of the spacetime; this allows us to more easily define operations involving them, and in the end, it will not matter since we will evaluate everything on the surface.

The projection operator can be applied to any tensor to isolate its components tangent to the surface. For instance, applied on the metric :

h_{\mu\nu}=\Pi^{\alpha}_{\mu}\Pi^{\beta}_{\nu}g_{\alpha\beta}=\Pi_{\mu\nu}\ .

(A.8)

This is another way to see the metric (
A.3
), as expressed in the embedding coordinates. One can verify we have of course
h_{ab}=t_{a}^{\mu}t_{b}^{\nu}h_{\mu\nu}
. Thus, the projection tensor and the surface metric are essentially interchangeable.

We have all we need to define Extrinsic curvature :

\displaystyle K_{\mu\nu}=\Pi^{\alpha}_{\mu}\Pi^{\beta}_{\nu}\nabla_{\alpha}n_{\beta}(=\Pi^{\alpha}_{\mu}\nabla_{\alpha}n_{\nu})\ .

(A.9)

Although it is not immediately obvious, one can show that
K_{\mu\nu}
is symmetric. This comes from the fact that locally, we can always write (
A.5
) by the implicit function theorem. In this form,
n_{\mu}
is locally hypersurface orthogonal, namely it satisfies
n_{[\rho}\nabla_{\mu}n_{\nu]}=0
. Contracting this with
n^{\rho}
yields
K_{[\mu\nu]}=0
as required.

Since
K_{\mu\nu}
is a tensor defined only on the surface, it is easier to work in the
s^{a}
coordinates :

\displaystyle K_{ab}=t^{\mu}_{a}t^{\nu}_{b}K_{\mu\nu}=t^{\mu}_{a}t^{\nu}_{b}\nabla_{\mu}n_{\nu}\ .

(A.10)

To compute
\nabla_{\mu}n_{\nu}
, one has to extend the vector field
n_{\nu}
to the full embedding space. Alternatively, expanding (
A.10
) :

\displaystyle K_{ab}=t^{\mu}_{a}t^{\nu}_{b}(\partial_{\mu}n_{\nu}+\Gamma^{\rho}_{\mu\nu}n_{\rho})=t^{\nu}_{b}\partial_{a}n_{\nu}+t^{\mu}_{a}t^{\nu}_{b}\Gamma^{\rho}_{\mu\nu}n_{\rho}\ .

(A.11)

With the notation (
A.11
), one does not need to go through the trouble of extending
n_{\nu}
; indeed, the embedding space derivative has been ”absorbed” by
t_{a}^{\mu}
, so that we need only take derivatives in the tangent directions, in which
n_{\mu}
is well-defined.

The trace of the extrinsic curvature that will enter the Gibbons-Hawking action is then simply
K=K_{ab}h^{ab}=K_{\mu\nu}h^{\mu\nu}
.

Appendix B
Thermal holographic interfaces

B.1
Extrinsic curvature formulas

In this section we collect some formulas for the extrinsic curvature of the membrane, in the parametrization:

\displaystyle x^{\mu}_{m}(\sigma,\tau)=(\tau,\sqrt{\sigma-M\ell^{2}},x_{m}(\sigma))\ ,

(B.1)

where the order of coordinates is
(t,r,x)
, and the metric (
2.20
). The tangent and normal (co)vector then are :

\displaystyle t_{\tau}^{\mu}

\displaystyle=(1,0,0)\ ,

(B.2)

\displaystyle t_{\sigma}^{\mu}

\displaystyle=(0,\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\sigma-M\ell^{2}}},x_{m}^{\prime}(\sigma))\ ,

\displaystyle n_{\mu}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}}(0,-x_{m}^{\prime}(\sigma),\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\sigma-M\ell^{2}}})\ ,

where
\mathcal{N}=\frac{1}{\ell}\sqrt{\frac{(r^{\prime})^{2}}{r^{2}}+(-M\ell^{2}+r^{2})(x^{\prime})^{2}}
is the normalisation of the normal vector. Note that we write
\sqrt{\sigma-M\ell^{2}}=r(\sigma)\equiv r
where useful.

Formula (
A.11
) gives us the Extrinsic curvature :

\displaystyle K_{\tau\tau}

\displaystyle=\frac{r(r^{2}-M\ell^{2})x^{\prime}(\sigma)}{\ell\sqrt{\frac{r^{\prime}{}^{2}\ell^{2}}{r^{2}}+(r^{2}-M\ell^{2})x^{\prime 2}}}=\frac{r^{2}x^{\prime}}{\ell}\times\sqrt{\frac{\sigma}{g(\sigma)}}\ ,

(B.3)

\displaystyle K_{\sigma\tau}

\displaystyle=0\ ,

\displaystyle K_{\sigma\sigma}

\displaystyle=\frac{x^{\prime}r^{\prime}{}^{2}\ell^{2}(3\sigma+M\ell^{2})+r\sigma x^{\prime}(r\sigma x^{\prime}{}^{2}+\ell^{2}r^{\prime\prime})+\frac{1}{2}\ell^{2}\sigma x^{\prime\prime}}{\ell\sigma\sqrt{\sigma g(\sigma)}}\ ,

where all
r
should be seen as function of
\sigma
. Note we used the shorthand
g(\sigma)
defined in (
2.22
). Then writing down the traced-reversed Israel condition (
2.18
), for the
\tau\tau
component, we find :

\displaystyle\frac{r^{2}_{1}x^{\prime}_{1}}{\ell_{1}}+\frac{r^{2}_{2}x^{\prime}_{2}}{\ell_{2}}=-\lambda\sqrt{\sigma g(\sigma)}\ ,

(B.4)

which is indeed the equation (
2.25
) used in the main text. The
\sigma\sigma
Israel condition is much more complex as seen from the lengthy formula (
B.3
) for
K_{\sigma\sigma}
. Nonetheless, one can write it down and plug in the solution (
2.37
), and after a computer-aided simplification, it indeed evaluates to
0=0
, such that it is redundant with the other equation.

B.2
Renormalized on-shell action

We reproduce here the Euclidean action of the holographic-interface model, in units
8\pi G=1
. It is the sum of bulk, brane, boundary and corner contributions, (see
[
224
]
for an explanation of the corner term)

\displaystyle S_{\rm gr}=-\frac{1}{2}

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{S}}_{1}}d^{3}x\sqrt{g_{1}}(R_{1}+\frac{2}{\ell_{1}^{2}})-\frac{1}{2}\int_{{\mathbb{S}}_{2}}d^{3}x\sqrt{g_{2}}(R_{2}+\frac{2}{\ell_{2}^{2}})+\lambda\int_{\mathbb{W}}d^{2}s\sqrt{h_{m}}

\displaystyle-\int_{\partial{\mathbb{S}}_{1}}d^{2}s\sqrt{h_{1}}K_{1}-\int_{\partial{\mathbb{S}}_{2}}d^{2}s\sqrt{h_{2}}K_{2}+\int_{\rm C}(\theta-\pi)\sqrt{h_{c}}+{\rm c.t.}\ ,

where the counterterms, abbreviated above by c.t., read
[
225
]

\displaystyle{\rm c.t.}=\frac{1}{\ell_{1}}\int_{\mathbb{B}_{1}}\sqrt{h_{1}}+\frac{1}{\ell_{2}}\int_{\mathbb{B}_{2}}\sqrt{h_{2}}-\int_{\mathbb{B}_{1}\cap\mathbb{B}_{2}}(\theta_{1}+\theta_{2})\sqrt{h_{c}}\ .

(B.5)

We remind that
\mathbb{S}_{j}
are the spacetime slices whose boundary is the sum of the asymptotic boundary
\mathbb{B}_{j}
and of the string worldsheet
\mathbb{M}
, i.e.
\partial\mathbb{S}_{j}=\mathbb{B}_{j}\cup\mathbb{M}
.

The induced metrics are denoted by the letter
h
. The
K_{j}
are the traces of the extrinsic curvatures on each slice computed with the outward-pointing normal vector. Finally, in addition to the standard Gibbons-Hawking-York boundary terms, one must add the Hayward term
[
226
,
224
]
at corners of
\partial\mathbb{S}_{j}
denoted by C.
1
1

1

These play no role here, but they can be important in the case of string junctions.

There is at least one such corner at the cutoff surface,
\mathbb{B}_{1}\cap\mathbb{B}_{2}
, where
\theta-\pi
is the sum of the angles
\theta_{j}
defined in figure
2.5
.

Let us break the action into an interior and a conformal boundary term,
S_{\rm gr}=S_{\rm int}+S_{\mathbb{B}}
, with the former including contributions from the worldsheet
\mathbb{M}
. Using the field equations
R_{j}=-{6/\ell_{j}^{2}}
and
K_{1}|_{\mathbb{M}}+K_{2}|_{\mathbb{M}}=2\lambda
, and the volume elements that follow from (
2.20
) and (
2.21
,
2.22
),

\sqrt{g_{j}}d^{3}x=\ell_{j}r_{j}dr_{j}dx_{j}dt\hskip 10.00002pt{\rm and}\hskip 10.00002pt\sqrt{h_{m}}d^{2}s=\sqrt{fg}d\sigma dt\ ,

(B.6)

We can write the interior on-shell action as follows :

S_{\rm int}=\frac{2}{\ell_{1}}\int_{{\Omega}_{1}}r_{1}\,dr_{1}dx_{1}dt+\frac{2}{\ell_{2}}\int_{{\Omega}_{2}}r_{2}dr_{2}dx_{2}dt-\lambda\int_{\mathbb{M}}\sqrt{fg}d\sigma dt\ .

(B.7)

We have been careful to distinguish the spacetime slice S
j
from the coordinate chart
\Omega_{j}
, because we will now use Stoke’s theorem treating
\Omega_{j}
as part of flat Euclidean space,

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1,2}\frac{2}{\ell_{j}}\int_{{\Omega}_{j}}r_{j}dr_{j}dx_{j}=\sum_{j=1,2}\frac{1}{\ell_{j}}\oint_{{\partial\Omega}_{j}}r_{j}^{2}(\hat{r}_{j}\cdot d\hat{n}_{j})\ ,

(B.8)

with
d\hat{n}_{j}dt
the surface element on the boundary
\partial\Omega_{j}
, and
\hat{r}_{j}
the unit vector in direction of increasing
r_{j}
. Crucially, the boundary of
\Omega_{j}
may include a horizon which is a regular interior submanifold of the Euclidean spacetime and is not therefore part of
\partial\mathbb{S}_{j}
. In particular, there is no Gibbons-Hawking-York contribution there.

The boundary integral in (
B.8
) receives contributions from the three pieces of
{\partial\Omega}_{1,2}
: the asymptotic cutoff surface
\mathbb{B}_{1}\cup\mathbb{B}_{2}
, the horizon if there is one, and the worldsheet
\mathbb{M}
.

Consider first the contributions from the asymptotic cutoff and horizon. The normal vector
\hat{n}_{j}
, in this case, is simply
\pm\hat{r}_{j}
, so the contribution is proportional to the size
\Delta x
of the boundary. For the membrane, the surface element in the Euclidean space
\Omega_{j}
is :

\displaystyle d\hat{n}_{j}=(-x_{j}^{\prime}(\sigma),r_{j}^{\prime}(\sigma))\ ,

(B.9)

where here this is the outgoing normal vector for one of the two membrane pieces. Both of them will contribute equally to the integral, we integrate on them in opposing directions, while also having opposing normal vectors. Combining this together yields :

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\ell_{j}}\oint_{{\partial\Omega}_{j}}r_{j}^{2}(\hat{r}_{j}\cdot d\hat{n}_{j})=\frac{r_{j}^{2}L_{j}}{\ell_{j}}-\frac{r_{h\,j}^{2}\Delta x_{j}\Bigl|_{Hor}}{\ell_{j}}-\int_{\sigma\in\Sigma}\frac{r^{2}_{j}(\sigma)x_{j}^{\prime}(\sigma)}{\ell_{j}}\ .

(B.10)

In the last term,
\Sigma
denotes the two ”half-membranes”, i.e. twice the interval
\sigma\in[\sigma_{+},\infty]
.

Very conveniently, this last term precisely cancels the third term in (
B.7
) by virtue of the Israel-Lanczos equation (
2.25
). In this way, we fortunately do not have to bother with integrals on the brane worldvolume.

Thus, after all the dust has settled, the action can be written as the sum of terms evaluated either at the black-hole horizon or at the cutoff. After integrating over periodic time (which simply contributes to a prefactor of
\frac{1}{T}
in front of the expressions) the interior part of the action, (
B.7
), reads

\displaystyle S_{\rm int}=\frac{r_{1}^{2}L_{1}}{\ell_{1}T}-\frac{M_{1}\ell_{1}\Delta x_{1}|_{\rm Hor}}{T}+\frac{r_{2}^{2}L_{2}}{\ell_{2}T}-\frac{M_{2}\ell_{2}\Delta x_{2}|_{\rm Hor}}{T}\ .

(B.11)

If the slice
\mathbb{S}_{j}
does not contain a horizon the corresponding contribution is absent,
\Delta x_{1}|_{\rm Hor}=0
. We wrote
r_{j}
for the cutoff radius, which is to be sent to infinity at the end. Note that we replaced
r_{hj}=M_{j}\ell_{j}^{2}
, the horizon radius.

We now turn to the conformal-boundary contributions from the lower line in the action (
B.2
). For a fixed-
r_{j}
surface, the outward-pointing unit normal expressed as a 1-form is
n
{}_{j}=dr_{j}/\sqrt{r_{j}^{2}-M_{j}\ell_{j}^{2}}
. Dropping the index
j
for simplicity, one finds after a little algebra (virtually identical to the computations of sec.
1.3.2
) :

\displaystyle K_{xx}=K_{tt}=-\frac{r}{\ell}\sqrt{r^{2}-M\ell^{2}}\Longrightarrow\sqrt{\hat{g}}K=-\frac{1}{\ell}(2r^{2}-M\ell^{2})\ .

(B.12)

Combining the Gibbons-Hawking-York terms and the counterterms gives

\displaystyle S_{\mathbb{B}}=\frac{1}{\ell_{1}T}(r_{1}\sqrt{r_{1}^{2}-M_{1}\ell_{1}^{2}}-2r_{1}^{2}+M_{1}\ell_{1}^{2})\Delta x_{1}\Bigl|_{\mathbb{B}_{1}}+(1\leftrightarrow 2)\ .

(B.13)

Expanding for large cutoff radius,
r_{j}|_{\mathbb{B}_{j}}\to\infty
, and dropping the terms that vanish in the limit we obtain

\displaystyle S_{\mathbb{B}}=\frac{1}{\ell_{1}T}(-r_{1}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}M_{1}\ell_{1}^{2})L_{1}+(1\rightarrow 2)\ .

(B.14)

Upon adding up (
B.11
) and (
B.14
) the leading divergent term cancels, giving the following result for the renormalized on-shell action:

\displaystyle S_{\rm gr}=\frac{M_{1}\ell_{1}}{2T}\bigl(L_{1}-2\Delta x_{1}\bigl|_{{\rm Hor}}\bigr)+\frac{M_{2}\ell_{2}}{2T}\bigl(L_{2}-2\Delta x_{2}\bigl|_{{\rm Hor}}\bigr)\ .

(B.15)

We used here the fact that
\Delta x_{j}|_{{\mathbb{B}}_{j}}=L_{j}
, and that
r_{j}^{2}=M_{j}\ell_{j}^{2}
at the horizon when one exists. We also used implicitly the fact that for smooth strings the Hayward term receives no contribution from the interior and is removed by the counterterm at the boundary.

As a check of this on-shell action let us compute the entropy. Using our formula for the internal energy
\langle E\rangle=\frac{1}{2}(M_{1}\ell_{1}L_{1}+M_{2}\ell_{2}L_{2})
, and
S_{\rm gr}=F/T=\langle E\rangle/T-S
we find

\displaystyle S

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{T}\bigl(M_{1}\ell_{1}\Delta x_{1}\bigl|_{{\rm Hor}}+M_{2}\ell_{2}\Delta x_{2}\bigl|_{{\rm Hor}}\bigr)\ ,

(B.16)

\displaystyle=4\pi^{2}T\bigl(\ell_{1}\Delta x_{1}\bigl|_{{\rm Hor}}+\ell_{2}\Delta x_{2}\bigl|_{{\rm Hor}}\bigr)=\frac{\mathcal{A}({\rm horizon})}{4G}\ .

In the lower line we used the fact that
M_{j}=(2\pi T)^{2}
and
r_{j}^{\rm H}=2\pi T\ell_{j}
for slices with horizon, plus our choice of units
8\pi G=1
. The calculation thus reproduces correctly the Bekenstein-Hawking entropy.

B.3
Opening arcs as elliptic integrals

In this appendix we express the opening arcs, (
2.38
-
2.40
), in terms of complete elliptic integrals of the first, second and third kind,

\displaystyle{\rm\bf K}(\nu)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\frac{dy}{\sqrt{(1-y^{2})(1-\nu y^{2})}}\ ,

(B.17)

\displaystyle{\bf E}(\nu)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\sqrt{1-\nu y^{2}}\,dy}{\sqrt{1-y^{2}}}\ ,

(B.18)

\displaystyle{\bf\Pi}(u,\nu)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\frac{dy}{(1-uy^{2})\sqrt{(1-y^{2})(1-\nu y^{2})}}\ .

(B.19)

Consider the boundary conditions(
2.38
). The other conditions (
2.39
,
2.40
) differ only by the constant periods or horizon arcs,
P_{j}
or
\Delta x_{j}|_{\rm hor}
.

Inserting the expression (
2.37
) for
x_{1}^{\prime}
gives

\displaystyle L_{1}=-\int_{\sigma_{+}}^{\infty}\frac{\ell_{1}\,d\sigma}{(\sigma+M_{1}\ell_{1}^{2})}\,\frac{(\lambda^{2}+\lambda_{0}^{2})\,\sigma+M_{1}-M_{2}}{\sqrt{A\sigma(\sigma-\sigma_{+})(\sigma-\sigma_{-})}}\ ,

(B.21)

and likewise for
L_{2}
. The roots
\sigma_{\pm}
are given by (
2.35
,
3.48
). We assume that we are not in the case
[H2, H2]
where
M_{1}=M_{2}>0
, nor in the fringe case
\sigma_{+}=-M_{j}\ell_{j}^{2}
when the string goes through an AdS center. These cases will be treated separately.

Separating the integral in two parts, and trading the integration variable
\sigma
for
y
, with
y^{2}:=\sigma_{+}/\sigma
, we obtain

\displaystyle L_{1}=-\frac{2\ell_{1}}{\sqrt{A\,\sigma_{+}}}\bigg[\frac{M_{1}-M_{2}}{M_{1}\ell_{1}^{2}}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{dy}{\sqrt{(1-y^{2})(1-\nu y^{2})}}

\displaystyle,

(B.22)

\displaystyle+\Bigl((\lambda^{2}+\lambda_{0}^{2})-\frac{M_{1}-M_{2}}{M_{1}\ell_{1}^{2}}\Bigr)\int_{0}^{1}\frac{y^{2}dy}{(1-u_{1}y^{2})\sqrt{(1-y^{2})(1-\nu y^{2})}}\bigg]

\displaystyle,

where
\nu=\sigma_{-}/\sigma_{+}
and
u_{1}=-M_{1}\ell_{1}^{2}/\sigma_{+}
. Identifying the elliptic integrals finally gives

\displaystyle L_{1}=-\frac{2\ell_{1}}{\sqrt{A\sigma_{+}}}\bigg[\frac{M_{1}-M_{2}}{M_{1}\ell_{1}^{\,2}}\Bigl({\rm\bf K}(\nu)-{\bf\Pi}(u_{1},\nu)\Bigr)+(\lambda^{2}+\lambda_{0}^{2}){\bf\Pi}(u_{1},\nu)\bigg]\ ,

(B.23)

and a corresponding expression for
L_{2}

\displaystyle L_{2}=-\frac{2\ell_{2}}{\sqrt{A\,\sigma_{+}}}\bigg[\frac{M_{2}-M_{1}}{M_{2}\ell_{2}^{\,2}}\,\Bigl({\rm\bf K}(\nu)-{\bf\Pi}(u_{2},\nu)\Bigr)+(\lambda^{2}-\lambda_{0}^{2})\,{\bf\Pi}(u_{2},\nu)\bigg]\ ,

(B.24)

with
u_{2}=-M_{2}\ell_{2}^{2}/\sigma_{+}
. The prefactors in (
B.23
) diverge when
M_{1}\to 0
but the singularity is removed by expanding
{\bf\Pi}(u_{1},\nu)
around
u_{1}=0
. In this limit

\displaystyle L_{1}(M_{1}=0)

\displaystyle=-\frac{2\ell_{1}}{\sqrt{A\,\sigma_{+}}}\bigg[\frac{M_{2}}{\sigma_{-}}({\rm\bf E}(\nu)-{\rm\bf K}(\nu))+(\lambda^{2}+\lambda_{0}^{2}){\rm\bf K}(\nu)\bigg]\ ,

(B.25)

\displaystyle L_{2}(M_{2}=0)

\displaystyle=-\frac{2\ell_{2}}{\sqrt{A\,\sigma_{+}}}\bigg[\frac{M_{1}}{\sigma_{-}}({\rm\bf E}(\nu)-{\rm\bf K}(\nu))+(\lambda^{2}-\lambda_{0}^{2}){\rm\bf K}(\nu)\bigg]\ ,

with
{\rm\bf E}(\nu)
the complete elliptic integral of the second kind.

The
M_{1}=M_{2}>0
geometries correspond to the high-temperature phase where
M_{j}=(2\pi T)^{2}
,
\sigma_{+}=0
and
\sigma_{-}=-(4\pi T\lambda)^{2}/A
. The integrals (
2.40
) simplify to elementary functions in this case. This seems to be related to the fact that in this case, the membrane metric becomes AdS
2
.:

\displaystyle L_{1}-\Delta_{1}^{\rm Hor}=-\frac{\ell_{1}(\lambda^{2}+\lambda_{0}^{2})}{\sqrt{A|\sigma_{-}|}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{ds}{(s+a)\sqrt{s+1}}=-\frac{\ell_{1}(\lambda^{2}+\lambda_{0}^{2})}{\sqrt{A|\sigma_{-}|}}\frac{2}{\sqrt{1-a}}{\rm arctanh}(\sqrt{1-a})\ ,

(B.26)

with
a=A\ell_{1}^{2}/4\lambda^{2}
. Using the expression (
2.35
) for
A
, and going through the samesteps for
j=2
, the expression greatly simplifies :

\displaystyle L_{1}-\Delta_{1}^{\rm Hor}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\pi T}\,{\rm tanh}^{-1}\left(\frac{\ell_{1}(\lambda^{2}+\lambda^{2}_{0})}{2\lambda}\right)\ ,

(B.27)

\displaystyle L_{2}-\Delta_{2}^{\rm Hor}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\pi T}\,{\rm tanh}^{-1}\left(\frac{\ell_{2}(\lambda^{2}-\lambda^{2}_{0})}{2\lambda}\right)\ .

Interestingly, since
\Delta_{2}^{\rm Hor}
must be positive,
TL_{2}
is bounded from below in the range
\lambda<\lambda_{0}
as discussed in section
2.6.1
.

In the high-temperature phase the on-shell action, (
B.15
), reads

\displaystyle I_{\rm gr}^{\rm(high-T)}=4\pi^{2}T\Bigl[-\frac{1}{2}(\ell_{1}L_{1}+\ell_{2}L_{2})+\ell_{1}(L_{1}-\Delta_{1}^{\rm Hor})+\ell_{2}(L_{2}-\Delta_{2}^{\rm Hor})\Bigr]\ .

(B.28)

Using the expressions (
B.27
) and rearranging the arc-tangent functions gives

\displaystyle I_{\rm gr}^{\rm(high-T)}:=\frac{E}{T}-S=-2\pi^{2}T(\ell_{1}L_{1}+\ell_{2}L_{2})-\log\,g_{I}\ ,

(B.29)

where the interface entropy
S=\log\,g_{I}
is given by (
2.31
). By this re-arrangement we are thus able to recover the interface entropy in a somewhat roundabout way, which nonetheless confirms the correctness of our solutions.

B.4
Sweeping is continuous

In this appendix, we show that sweeping transitions are continuous. We focus for definiteness on the sweeping of the
j=2
AdS center at zero temperature (all other cases work out the same). The transition takes place when
\mu
crosses the critical value
\mu_{2}^{*}
given by eq. (
2.64
). Setting
\mu=\mu_{2}^{*}(1-\delta)
in expression (
2.52
) gives

\displaystyle f_{2}(\mu)=\frac{\ell_{2}}{\sqrt{A}}\int_{s_{+}}^{\infty}ds\frac{(\lambda^{2}-\lambda_{0}^{2})(s-\mu\ell_{2}^{2})\,+\delta}{(s-\mu\ell_{2}^{2})\sqrt{As(s-s_{+})(s-s_{-})}}\ ,

(B.30)

\displaystyle=\frac{2\ell_{2}(\lambda^{2}-\lambda_{0}^{2})}{\sqrt{As_{+}}}\,{\bf K}\bigl(\frac{s_{-}}{s_{+}}\bigr)\,+\,\frac{\ell_{2}\,\delta}{\sqrt{A}}\,\underbrace{\int_{s_{+}}^{\infty}\frac{ds}{(s-\mu\ell_{2}^{2})\sqrt{s(s-s_{+})(s-s_{-})}}}_{J}\ .

(B.31)

The first term is continuous at
\delta=0
, but the second requires some care because the integral
J
diverges. This is because for small
\delta

\displaystyle s_{+}-\mu\ell_{2}^{2}=\ \frac{\delta^{2}}{4\lambda^{2}\mu_{2}^{*}}+\mathcal{O}(\delta^{3})\ ,

(B.32)

as one finds by explicit computation of the expression (
2.53
). we set
\delta=0
,
J
diverges near the lower integration limit. To bring the singular behavior to
0
we perform the change of variable
u^{2}=s-s_{+}
, so that

\displaystyle J=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{2du}{(u^{2}+\delta^{2}/4\lambda^{2}\mu_{2}^{*})\sqrt{(u^{2}+s_{+}^{*})(u^{2}+s_{+}^{*}-s_{-}^{*})}}\ ,

(B.33)

where we kept only the leading order in
\delta
, and
s_{\pm}^{*}
are the roots at
\mu=\mu_{2}^{*}
. Since
s_{+}^{*}
and
s_{+}^{*}-s_{-}^{*}
are positive and finite, the small-
\delta
behavior of the integral is (after rescaling appropriately
u
)

\displaystyle J=\frac{4\lambda|\mu_{2}^{*}|}{|\delta|\sqrt{s_{+}^{*}(s_{+}^{*}-s_{-}^{*})}}\underbrace{\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{du}{u^{2}+1}}_{\pi/2}+{\rm finite}\ .

(B.34)

Inserting in expression (
B.31
) and doing some tedious algebra leads finally to a discontinuity of the function
f_{2}(\mu)
equal to sign
(\delta)\pi/\sqrt{\mu_{2}^{*}}
. This is precisely what is required for
L_{2}
, (
2.50
), to be continuous when the red (
j=2
) slice goes from type
E1
at negative
\delta
to type
E2
at positive
\delta
.

This, however, does not tell us anything about the continuity of higher derivatives of
L_{2}(\mu)
. The expansions that one needs to make become exponentially bigger, and it becomes nigh impossible to keep track of all the terms, as even Mathematica struggles to perform the simplifications. However, we made a numerical analysis with several numerical parameters. What this analysis revealed is that the continuity of the Free energy seems to extend at least to the third derivative (going deeper produced numerical instabilities which made it hard to conclude). For this reason, it is safe to say that the transition is completely smooth in terms of Free energy and its derivatives. It is thus a phase transition of another nature.

B.5
Bubbles exist

We show here that the bubble phenomenon of section
2.8
is indeed realized in a region of the parameter space of the holographic model.

This is the region of non-degenerate gravitational vacua (
\ell_{2}
strictly bigger than
\ell_{1}
) and a sufficiently light domain wall. Specifically, we will show that for
\lambda
close to its minimal value,
\lambda_{\rm min}
, the arc
L_{1}(\mu=0)
is negative, so the wall self-intersects and
\mu_{0}
is necessarily finite.

Let
\lambda=\lambda_{\rm min}(1+\delta)
with
\delta\ll 1
. Setting
\mu=0
and expanding eqs. (
2.53
) for small
\delta
gives

\displaystyle A=\frac{8\lambda_{\rm min}^{2}\,\delta}{\ell_{1}\ell_{2}}+\mathcal{O}(\delta^{2}),\hskip 10.00002pts_{+}=\frac{\ell_{2}}{4\lambda_{\rm min}}+\mathcal{O}(\delta),\hskip 10.00002pts_{-}=-\frac{\ell_{1}}{\ 2\lambda_{\rm min}\delta}+\mathcal{O}(1)\ .

(B.35)

Plugging into (
B.23
) with
M_{2}=\mu M_{1}\approx 0
we find :

\sqrt{|M_{1}|}L_{1}=-\frac{2}{\ell_{1}\sqrt{As_{+}}}\left[{\bf K}\left(\frac{s_{-}}{s_{+}}\right)+(1-\frac{2\ell_{1}}{\ell_{2}}){\bf\Pi}\left({\ell_{1}^{2}}{s_{+}},\,\frac{s_{-}}{s_{+}}\right)\right]\ ,

(B.36)

where we have only kept leading orders in
\delta
. Now we need the asymptotic form of the elliptic integrals when their argument diverges

{\bf K}\left[-\frac{a}{\delta}\right]\approx{\bf\Pi}\left[u,-\frac{a}{\delta}\right]\approx-\frac{\ln(\delta)\sqrt{\delta}}{2\sqrt{a}}+\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{\delta})\ ,

(B.37)

for
\delta\to 0_{+}
with
u,a
fixed. Using
a=2\ell_{1}/\ell_{2}
finally gives

\displaystyle\sqrt{|M_{1}|}L_{1}\approx\bigl(\frac{\ell_{2}}{\ell_{1}}-1\bigr)^{1/2}\ln(\delta)+{\rm subleading}\ .

(B.38)

For
\delta\ll 1
this is negative, proving our claim. Note that we took the green slice to be of type
E2
, as follows from our analysis of the sweeping transitions for light domain walls – see section
2.7.2
.

Appendix C
Steady holographic interfaces

C.1
Extrinsic curvature formulas

In this section we collect some formulas for the extrinsic curvature of the membrane, in the parametrisation :

\displaystyle x^{\mu}_{m}(\sigma,\tau)=(\tau+f(\sigma),\sqrt{\sigma-M\ell^{2}},x_{m}(\sigma))\ ,

(C.1)

where the order of coordinates is
(t,r,x)
, and the metric (
3.1
). The tangent and normal (co)vector then are :

\displaystyle t_{\tau}^{\mu}

\displaystyle=(1,0,0)\ ,

(C.2)

\displaystyle t_{\sigma}^{\mu}

\displaystyle=(f^{\prime}(\sigma),\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\sigma-M\ell^{2}}},x_{m}^{\prime}(\sigma))\ ,

\displaystyle n_{\mu}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathcal{N}}(0,-x_{m}^{\prime}(\sigma),\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\sigma-M\ell^{2}}})\ ,

where
\mathcal{N}=\sqrt{\frac{(r^{\prime})^{2}}{r^{2}}\left(\frac{\sigma}{h(r)}\right)+h(r)\frac{(x^{\prime})^{2}}{\ell^{2}}}
is the normalisation of the normal vector. Note that we write
\sigma-M\ell^{2}=r^{2}(\sigma)\equiv r^{2}
where useful, and we used the definition (
3.2
) for
h(r)
.

Note that the tangent vector is independent from
f(\sigma)
, this is expected as it does not change the geometry of the membrane embedding. It will however have an impact on the matching conditions.

Formula (
A.11
) gives us the Extrinsic curvature :

\displaystyle K_{\tau\tau}

\displaystyle=\frac{rx^{\prime}(\sigma)h(r)}{\ell^{2}\sqrt{\frac{r^{\prime}{}^{2}\sigma}{r^{2}h(r)}+\frac{h(r)x^{\prime 2}}{\ell^{2}}}}=\frac{r^{2}x^{\prime}(\sigma)h(r)}{\ell\sqrt{\sigma}\sqrt{|\hat{g}|}}\ ,

(C.3)

\displaystyle K_{\sigma\tau}

\displaystyle=\frac{\frac{rf^{\prime}(\sigma)x^{\prime}(\sigma)h(r)}{\ell^{2}}-\frac{J\ell x^{\prime}(\sigma)}{2rh(r)}}{\sqrt{\frac{r^{\prime}{}^{2}\sigma}{r^{2}h(r)}+\frac{h(r)x^{\prime 2}}{\ell^{2}}}}=\frac{\frac{r^{2}x^{\prime}h(r)f^{\prime}}{\ell}-\frac{J\ell^{2}x^{\prime}}{2h(r)}}{\sqrt{\sigma}\sqrt{|\hat{g}|}}\ ,

where all
r
should be seen as function of
\sigma
. Note we used the shorthand
\hat{g}={\rm det}(\hat{g})
defined in (
3.29
). We did not include the equation for
K_{\sigma\sigma}
as it is very cumbersome, and is not used anywhere in the main-text.

Then writing down the traced-reversed Israel condition (
2.18
). We indeed find (
3.33
) and (
3.34
) as in the main-text. As for the
(\sigma\sigma)
equation, it can be shown with alot of handwork that it is indeed satisfied when the other two are.

C.2
Horizon inequalities

In section
3.6
we asserted that BTZ geometries whose ergoregions can be glued together by a thin brane obey the inequalities

\displaystyle\sigma_{+}^{{\rm H}1}

\displaystyle>\sigma_{+}^{{\rm H}2}\hskip 10.00002pt{\rm if}M_{1}>M_{2}\ ,

(C.4)

\displaystyle\sigma_{+}^{{\rm H}2}

\displaystyle<\sigma_{+}^{{\rm H}1}\hskip 10.00002pt{\rm if}M_{1}<M_{2}\ ,

where the horizon locations are

\displaystyle\sigma_{\pm}^{{\rm H}j}=-\frac{M_{j}\ell_{j}^{2}}{2}\pm\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{M_{j}^{2}\ell_{j}^{4}-J^{2}\ell_{j}^{2}}\ ,

(C.5)

and
J\equiv|J_{1}|=|J_{2}|>0
. This ordering of the outer horizons is manifest if one expands at the leading order for small
J
. We want to show that it is valid for all values of
J
.

If as
J
is cranked up the ordering was at some point reversed, then we would have
\sigma_{+}^{{\rm H}1}=\sigma_{+}^{{\rm H}2}
, or equivalently

\displaystyle M_{2}\ell_{2}^{2}-M_{1}\ell_{1}^{2}=\sqrt{M_{2}^{2}\ell_{2}^{4}-J^{2}\ell_{2}^{2}}-\sqrt{M_{1}^{2}\ell_{1}^{4}-J^{2}\ell_{1}^{2}}\ .

(C.6)

Squaring twice to eliminate the square roots gives

J^{2}=\frac{4\ell_{1}^{2}\ell_{2}^{2}(M_{1}-M_{2})(M_{2}\ell_{2}^{2}-M_{1}\ell_{1}^{2})}{(\ell_{2}^{2}-\ell_{1}^{2})^{2}}\ .

(C.7)

Without loss of generality we assume, as elsewhere in the text, that
\ell_{1}\leq\ell_{2}
. If
M_{2}>M_{1}
, then automatically
M_{2}\ell_{2}^{2}>M_{1}\ell_{1}^{2}
and (
C.7
) has no solution for real
J
. In this case the ordering (
C.4
) cannot be reversed.

If on the other hand
M_{1}>M_{2}
and
M_{2}\ell_{2}^{2}-M_{1}\ell_{1}^{2}>0
we need to work harder. Inserting
J^{2}
from (
C.7
) back in the original equation (
C.6
) gives after rearrangements

\displaystyle(\ell_{2}^{2}-\ell_{1}^{2})(M_{2}\ell_{2}^{2}-M_{1}\ell_{1}^{2})=

\displaystyle\ell_{2}^{2}\bigl|(M_{2}\ell_{2}^{2}-M_{1}\ell_{1}^{2})-\ell_{1}^{2}(M_{1}-M_{2})\bigr|\ ,

(C.8)

\displaystyle-\ell_{1}^{2}\left|(M_{2}\ell_{2}^{2}-M_{1}\ell_{1}^{2})-\ell_{2}^{2}(M_{1}-M_{2})\right|\ ,

where the absolute values come from the square roots. This equation is not automatically obeyed whenever its doubly-squared version is. A solution only exists if

M_{1}-M_{2}\leq\frac{\ell_{2}^{2}M_{2}-\ell_{1}^{2}M_{1}}{\ell_{2}^{2}}\Leftrightarrow\frac{M_{1}}{M_{2}}\leq\frac{2\ell_{2}^{2}}{\ell_{2}^{2}+\ell_{1}^{2}}\ .

(C.9)

Remember now that we only care about solutions with walls in the ergoregion, for which
M_{1}-M_{2}=\lambda J
, see eq.(
3.53
). Plugging in (
C.7
) this gives

M_{2}=\bigl[1-\frac{4\lambda_{0}^{2}\\
lam^{2}}{\lambda_{0}^{4}+4{\lambda^{2}}/{\ell_{1}^{2}}}\bigr]M_{1}\ ,

(C.10)

with
\lambda_{0}^{2}=(\ell_{2}^{2}-\ell_{1}^{2})/\ell_{1}^{2}\ell_{2}^{2}
, see eq.(
2.30
). Consistency with the bound (
C.9
) for a brane tension in the allowed range then requires

\lambda_{\rm min}<\lambda\leq\frac{\ell_{1}\lambda_{0}^{2}}{2}\ ,

(C.11)

where
\lambda_{\rm min}=(\ell_{2}-\ell_{1})/\ell_{1}\ell_{2}
. As one can easily check, this implies
\ell_{1}>\ell_{2}
which contradicts our initial assumption. We conclude that (
C.6
) has no solution, and the ordering (
C.4
) holds for all
J
.

For completeness, let us also consider the ordering of the inner horizons. Clearly
\sigma_{+}^{{\rm H}j}>\sigma_{-}^{{\rm H}j}
always, and for small
J
also
\sigma_{+}^{{\rm H}1}>\sigma_{-}^{{\rm H}2}
and
\sigma_{+}^{{\rm H}2}>\sigma_{-}^{{\rm H}1}
. To violate these last inequalities we need
\sigma_{+}^{{\rm H}1}=\sigma_{-}^{{\rm H}2}
or
\sigma_{+}^{{\rm H}2}=\sigma_{-}^{{\rm H}1}
for some finite
J
, or equivalently

\displaystyle M_{2}\ell_{2}^{2}-M_{1}\ell_{1}^{2}=\mp\bigl(\sqrt{M_{2}^{2}\ell_{2}^{4}-J^{2}\ell_{2}^{2}}+\sqrt{M_{1}^{2}\ell_{1}^{4}-J^{2}\ell_{1}^{2}}\,\bigr)\ .

(C.12)

Squaring twice gives back eq.(
C.7
) which has no solution if
M_{2}>M_{1}
. But if
M_{1}>M_{2}
and
M_{2}\ell_{2}^{2}-M_{1}\ell_{1}^{2}>0
, solutions to
\sigma_{+}^{{\rm H}2}=\sigma_{-}^{{\rm H}1}
cannot be ruled out. Indeed, inserting
J
from (
C.7
) in (
C.12
) with the
+
sign gives

\displaystyle(\ell_{2}^{2}-\ell_{1}^{2})(M_{2}\ell_{2}^{2}-M_{1}\ell_{1}^{2})=

\displaystyle\ell_{2}^{2}\bigl|(M_{2}\ell_{2}^{2}-M_{1}\ell_{1}^{2})-\ell_{1}^{2}(M_{1}-M_{2})\bigr|\ ,

(C.13)

\displaystyle+\ell_{1}^{2}\left|(M_{2}\ell_{2}^{2}-M_{1}\ell_{1}^{2})-\ell_{2}^{2}(M_{1}-M_{2})\right|\ ,

which requires that

\displaystyle\frac{\ell_{2}^{2}M_{2}-\ell_{1}^{2}M_{1}}{\ell_{2}^{2}}\leq M_{1}-M_{2}\leq\frac{\ell_{2}^{2}M_{2}-\ell_{1}^{2}M_{1}}{\ell_{1}^{2}}\ .

(C.14)

These conditions are compatible with
M_{1}-M_{2}=\lambda J
and
\lambda
in the allowed range, so the outer horizon of slice 2 need not always come before the Cauchy horizon of slice 1.

Finally one may ask if the inner (Cauchy) horizons can join continuously, i.e. if
\sigma_{-}^{{\rm H}1}=\sigma_{-}^{{\rm H}2}
is allowed. A simple calculation shows that this is indeed possible for
\ell_{2}/\ell_{1}<3
, a critical ratio of central charges that also arose in references
[
77
,
189
]
. We don’t know if this is a coincidence, or if some deeper reason lurks below.

C.3
Background on flowing funnels

In this appendix, we collect some formulae on the flowing funnels discussed in section
3.6.3
. We start with the most general asymptotically-locally-AdS
3
solution in Fefferman-Graham coordinates, generalizing the Banados geometries to arbitrary boundary metric, see
1.146
, but here
g_{(0)}
will be arbitrary instead of flat :

\displaystyle ds^{2}=\frac{\ell^{2}dz^{2}}{z^{2}}+\frac{1}{z^{2}}g_{\alpha\beta}(x,z)dx^{\alpha}dx^{\beta}\ ,

(C.15)

where
g_{\alpha\beta}
is a quartic polynomial in
z
(written here as a matrix)

\displaystyle g(x,z)=g_{(0)}+z^{2}g_{(2)}+\frac{z^{4}}{4}g_{(2)}g_{(0)}^{-1}g_{(2)}\ .

(C.16)

In this equation
g_{(0)}
is the boundary metric and
g_{(2)}
is given by (see sec.
1.7
for more details :

\displaystyle g_{(2)\;\alpha\beta}=\frac{\ell^{2}}{2}R_{(0)}g_{(0)\alpha\beta}+{\ell}\langle T_{\alpha\beta}\rangle\ ,

(C.17)

where
R_{(0)}
is the Ricci scalar of
g_{(0)}
, and
\langle T_{\alpha\beta}\rangle
the expectation value of the energy-momentum tensor.
This must be conserved,
\nabla^{a}_{(0)}\langle T_{ab}\rangle=0
, and should obey the trace anomaly equation
g_{(0)}^{ab}\langle T_{ab}\rangle=-(c/24\pi)R_{(0)}
.

We may take the boundary metric to be that of the Schwarzschild black hole
(this differs from the metric in
[
170
]
, but since it is not dynamical we are free to choose our preferred
boundary metric),

\displaystyle ds^{2}_{(0)}=-f(x)\,dt^{2}+\frac{dx^{2}}{f(x)}\hskip 20.00003pt{\rm with}\hskip 10.00002ptf(x)=\frac{x}{x+a}\ .

(C.18)

The horizon at
x=0
has temperature
\Theta_{S}=(4\pi a)^{-1}
. Using the familiar tortoise coordinates we can write

\displaystyle ds^{2}_{(0)}=f(x)(-dt^{2}+dx_{*}^{2})\hskip 20.00003pt{\rm where}\hskip 10.00002ptx_{*}=x+a\log x\ .

(C.19)

Let
w^{\pm}=x_{*}\pm t
.
The expectation value
of the energy-momentum tensor in the black-hole metric can be expressed in terms of
\phi=\log f(x)

as follows

\displaystyle\langle T_{\pm\pm}\rangle=\frac{\ell}{2}\,\big[\partial^{2}_{\pm}\phi-\frac{1}{2}(\partial_{\pm}\phi)^{2}\big]+k_{\pm}(w^{\pm})\ ,\hskip 10.00002pt\langle T_{+-}\rangle=-\frac{\ell}{2}\,\partial_{+}\partial_{-}\phi\ ,

(C.20)

with
k_{\pm}
arbitrary functions of
w^{\pm}
that depend on the choice of state. At
x\gg a
where the metric is flat,
k_{\pm}
determines the incoming and outgoing fluxes of energy. In a stationary solution, these must be constant. If a heat bath at temperature
\Theta_{+}
is placed at infinity,
k_{+}=\pi^{2}\ell\Theta_{+}^{2}
. The function
k_{-}
, on the other hand, is fixed by requiring that there is no outgoing flux at the Schwarzschild horizon.

From

\displaystyle\frac{\ell}{2}\big[\partial^{2}_{\pm}\phi-\frac{1}{2}(\partial_{\pm}\phi)^{2}\big]=-\frac{\ell(a^{2}+4ax)}{16(x+a)^{4}}\ ,

(C.21)

we deduce
\langle T_{--}\rangle|_{x=0}=0\Longrightarrow k_{-}=\ell/16a^{2}=\pi^{2}\ell\Theta_{S}^{2}
. The outgoing flux at infinity is thermalized at the black hole temperature, as expected.

Inserting the expressions (
C.17
-
C.21
) in (
C.15
) and (
C.16
) gives the flowing-funnel metric in Fefferman-Graham coordinates. These are however singular coordinates, not well adapted for calculating the event horizon as shown in
[
170
]
. Following this reference, one can compute the horizon by going to BTZ coordinates – this is possible because all solutions are locally equivalent in three dimensions. The change from any metric (
C.15
) - (
C.16
) to local BTZ coordinates has been worked out in ref.
[
227
]
(see also
[
228
]
) and can be used to compute the black-funnel shapes. A noteworthy feature is that the funnels start vertically inwards at
x=0

[
170
]
making a delta-function contribution to the area density. Note that figure
3.7
shows two independent flowing funnels with Schwarzschild temperatures
\Theta_{S}=\Theta_{1}^{\rm eff}

and
\Theta_{2}^{\rm eff}
.

Appendix D
RT surfaces and entanglement entropy

D.1
Euclidean construction for single-crossing geodesic

We use fig.
4.5
and aim to express the depicted points as a function of
\lambda
(where
O_{2}=(\lambda,0)
),
\varphi
and
\theta_{i}
. The basic equation we will use is the law of sines inside various triangles. We will work with fig.
4.5
as a reference, but if one is careful the formulas are applicable also to situations not depicted by the figure, for instance, when
\lambda>0
. When writing something like
O_{1}O_{2}
, we mean the length between the segment
[O_{1},O_{2}]
. This in particular could become negative in the formulas we will give, signaling that
O_{1}
and
O_{2}
cross. This is always accompanied by a sign change in the opposing angle so that the law of sines still works.

By the triangle
(O_{2},O,X)
, we have :

\displaystyle\frac{O_{2}X}{\sin(\th_2)}=\frac{OO_{2}}{\sin(\th_2-\phi)}\Leftrightarrow O_{2}X=\frac{(-\lambda)\sin(\th_2)}{\sin(\th_2-\phi)}\ ,

(D.1)

where we use
OO_{2}=-\lambda
.

Then we find
OO_{1}
using the triangle
(O,O_{2},O_{1})
:

\displaystyle OO_{1}=\frac{OO_{2}\sin(\pi-\phi)}{\sin(\phi-\th_1)}=\frac{(-\lambda)\sin(\phi)}{\sin(\phi-\th_1)}\ .

(D.2)

The last piece we need is
O_{1}X
, using the triangle
(O_{1},X,O)
:

\displaystyle O_{1}X=\frac{OO_{1}\sin(\pi-\th_2+\th_1)}{\sin(\th_2-\phi)}\ .

(D.3)

With that in mind we can compute the anchor points
\sigma_{1}
,
\sigma_{2}
, as well as
Ox
as we should require it to be positive :

\displaystyle O\sigma_{2}

\displaystyle=O_{2}X-OO_{2}=(-\lambda)\left(\frac{\sin(\th_2)}{\sin(\th_2-\phi)}-1\right)\ ,

(D.4)

\displaystyle O\sigma_{1}

\displaystyle=OO_{1}+O_{1}X=\frac{(-\lambda)\sin(\phi)}{\sin(\phi-\th_1)}\left(1+\frac{\sin(\th_2-\th_1)}{\sin(\th_2-\phi)}\right)\ ,

\displaystyle OX

\displaystyle=\frac{(-\lambda)\sin(\phi)}{\cos(\th_2-\phi)}\ .

We can re-express these in terms of the initial angles with the identities
\theta_{2}=\frac{\pi}{2}+\psi_{2}
and
\theta_{1}=\psi_{1}+\psi_{2}
.

\displaystyle O\sigma_{2}

\displaystyle=(-\lambda)\left(\frac{\cos(\psi_2)}{\cos(\psi_2-\phi)}-1\right)\ ,

(D.5)

\displaystyle O\sigma_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{(-\lambda)\sin(\phi)}{\sin(\phi-\psi_2-\psi_1)}\left(1+\frac{\cos(\psi_1)}{\cos(\psi_2-\phi)}\right)\ ,

\displaystyle OX

\displaystyle=\frac{(-\lambda)\sin(\phi)}{\cos(\phi-\psi_2)}\ .

Then, by replacing
\phi-\psi_{2}\equiv\alpha
, we obtain the equation (
4.29
) used in the main-text.

We would like now to compute the length of this geodesic. We will apply the formula (
4.33
) to the two pieces composing the geodesic. When the geodesic is anchored at the boundary, we cut it off at
z_{i}=\epsilon_{i}
. Denoting
R
the radius of the semi-circle, the initial angle
\theta_{0}
is given by :

\displaystyle\frac{\epsilon_{0}}{R}=\sin(\al_0)\approx\alpha_{0}\ .

(D.6)

Thus because of the presence of the cutoffs, the length of the geodesic does depend on its radius, unlike what was suggested by the formula (
4.33
).

The semi-circle going from
\sigma_{2}\rightarrow x
has radius
R_{2}
given by :

\displaystyle R_{2}=O_{2}\sigma_{2}=\frac{(-\lambda)\cos(\psi_2)}{\cos(\al)}\ .

(D.7)

The starting angle for this portion is given by the cutoff
\epsilon_{2}
, while
\theta_{f}=\phi
. This gives the length
L_{2}
of this portion :

\displaystyle L_{2}=\ell_{2}\ln(\frac{2R_2\sin(\al+\psi_2)}{\eps_2(1+\cos(\al+\psi_2))})=\ell_{2}\ln(\frac{2R_2}{\eps_2}\tan(\frac{\al+\psi_2}{2}))\ ,

(D.8)

keeping only the leading order in
\epsilon_{2}
.

The other semi-circle from
X\rightarrow\sigma_{1}
has radius
R_{1}
:

\displaystyle R_{1}=O_{1}X=\frac{(-\lambda)\cos(\psi_1)\sin(\al+\psi_2)}{\sin(\al-\psi_1)\cos(\al)}\ ,

(D.9)

while the initial and final angles are
\theta_{0}=\alpha-\psi_{1}
and
\theta_{f}=\frac{\epsilon_{1}}{R_{1}}
. Then its length
L_{1}
is :

\displaystyle L_{1}=\ell_{1}\ln(\frac{2R_1(1+\cos(\al-\psi_1))}{\eps_1\sin(\al-\psi_1)})=\ell_{1}\ln(\frac{2R_1}{\eps_1}\frac{1}{\tan(\frac{\al-\psi_1}{2})})\ .

(D.10)

The full length of the geodesic is obtained simply by summing the two contributions,
L=L_{1}+L_{2}
. After some algebra and re-arranging, we obtain the formula (
4.32
) used in the main-text.

D.2
Euclidean construction for double-crossing geodesic

We use fig.
4.6
to express the depicted points as a function of
\lambda
(where
O_{2}=(\lambda,0)
),
\varphi
and the
\theta_{i}
. As in the previous section, this is done by repeated use of the law of sines.

Beginning with the triangle
(O,O_{1},O_{3})
, we have :

\displaystyle OO_{2}=\frac{(-\lambda)\sin(\phi)}{\sin(\th_1-\phi)}\ .

(D.11)

With the triangle
(O,O_{2},O_{3})
:

\displaystyle OO_{3}=OO_{2}\frac{\sin(2\th_2-\th_1-\phi)}{\sin(2\th_2-\phi)}=-\frac{\lambda\sin(\phi)\sin(2\th_2-\th_1-\phi)}{\sin(\th_1-\phi)\sin(2\th_2-\phi)}\ .

(D.12)

To find
\sigma_{2}
, we need the radius of the semi-circle centered at
O_{3}
. We use the triangle
(O,O_{3},X_{2})
:

\displaystyle O_{3}X_{2}=OO_{3}\frac{\sin(\th_2)}{\sin(\th_2-\phi)}\ ,

(D.13)

where
OO_{3}
is given by (
D.13
). Then
O\sigma_{2}=OO_{3}+O_{3}X_{2}
:

\displaystyle O\sigma_{2}

\displaystyle=OO_{3}(1+\frac{\sin(\th_2)}{\sin(\th_2-\phi)})\ .

(D.14)

Similarly,
O\sigma_{1}=O_{1}\sigma_{1}-OO_{1}=O_{1}X_{1}-OO_{1}
. We use triangle
(O,O_{1},X_{1})
:

\displaystyle O_{1}X_{1}

\displaystyle=(-\lambda)\left(1-\frac{\sin(\th_2)}{\sin(\th_2-\phi)}\right)\ ,

(D.15)

\displaystyle O\sigma_{1}

\displaystyle=(-\lambda)\left(\frac{\sin(\th_2)}{\sin(\th_2-\phi)}\right)\ .

Collecting the expression and replacing with the angles
\psi_{i}
:

\displaystyle O\sigma_{1}

\displaystyle=(-\lambda)\left(\frac{\cos(\psi_2)}{\cos(\psi_2 - \phi)}-1\right)\ ,

(D.16)

\displaystyle O\sigma_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{(-\lambda)\sin(\phi)}{\sin(\psi_1+\psi_2-\phi)}\frac{\sin(\phi+\psi_1-\psi_2)}{\sin(\phi-2\psi_2)}\left(1+\frac{\cos(\psi_2)}{\cos(\psi_2 - \phi)}\right)\ ,

which yield the formulas (
4.36
) when doing the change of variables
\alpha=\phi-\psi_{2}
. In what follows, we express everythin in terms of
\alpha
instead of
\phi
.

For the Length of the geodesics, we must now compute the length of three separate segments, using the formula (
4.33
). Some extra angles need to be determined on fig.
4.6
, but we don’t details that here.

Consider first the segment connecting
\sigma_{1}\rightarrow X_{1}
. The radius is given by
R_{1}
:

\displaystyle R_{1}=O_{1}X_{1}=\frac{(-\lambda)\cos(\psi_2)}{\cos(al)}\ .

(D.17)

The starting angle
\theta_{0}
is given by the cutoff,
\epsilon_{1}
, while the ending
\theta_{f}=\alpha+\psi_{2}
.

This gives a contribution to the length :

\displaystyle L_{1}=\ell_{2}\ln\left(\frac{2R_{1}}{\epsilon_{1}}\tan\left(\frac{\alpha+\psi_{2}}{2}\right)\right)\ .

(D.18)

For the second segment
X_{1}\rightarrow X_{2}
, the Radius of the geodesic is irrelevant. The initial angle is
\theta_{0}=\pi-(\alpha+\psi_{1})
, final
\theta_{f}=\psi-(\psi_{1}-\alpha)
. This gives the contribution
L_{3}
:

\displaystyle L_{3}=\ell_{1}\ln\left(\frac{\tan(\frac{\al+\psi_1}{2})}{\tan(\frac{\psi_1-\al}{2})}\right)\ .

(D.19)

For the last segment
L_{2}
, the radius
R_{2}
is :

\displaystyle R_{3}=O_{3}X_{2}=OO_{3}\frac{\sin(\th_2)}{\sin(\th_2-\phi)}=\frac{-\lambda\sin(\al+\psi_2)\sin(\al+\psi_1)}{\sin(\psi_1-\al)\sin(\al-\psi_2)}\frac{\cos(\psi_2)}{\cos(\al)}\ .

(D.20)

The corresponding length
L_{2}
is then :

\displaystyle L_{3}=\ell_{2}\ln(\frac{2R_3}{\eps_2\tan(\frac{\al-\psi_2}{2})})\ .

(D.21)

Adding the contributions of the
L_{i}
gives the length of the full geodesic. After re-arranging, we obtain (
4.40
) used in the main-text.

D.3
Peculiar geodesics in the spinning black string geometry

In this section we briefly look at the shape of the different spacelike geodesics in the spinning string geometry. In particular, we highlight the fact that there are horizon entering geodesics, but that they cannot have two anchors on the boundary.

The geodesics are obtained from the equations of sec.
4.1
, composed with the change of coordinates (
4.54
). Consider first the type
K_{+}K_{-}>0
, described in sec.
4.1.1
. In poincaré space, this geodesic has two anchor points on the boundary. However, this does not necessarily translate to Finkelstein coordinates. To see this, it is useful to consider the inverse of (
4.54
) (for
J>0
) :

\displaystyle r^{2}

\displaystyle=r_{+}^{2}+\frac{(w_{+}w_{-})(r_{+}^{2}-r_{-}^{2})}{z_{p}^{2}}\ ,

(D.22)

\displaystyle v

\displaystyle=\frac{\ell}{2}\left(\frac{1}{r_{+}-r_{-}}\ln(\frac{w_+(r+r_-)}{r+r_+})-\frac{1}{r_{+}+r_{-}}\ln(\frac{w_-(r+r_-)}{r-r_+})\right)\ ,

\displaystyle y

\displaystyle=\frac{\ell}{2}\left(\frac{1}{r_{+}-r_{-}}\ln(\frac{w_+(r+r_-)}{r+r_+})+\frac{1}{r_{+}+r_{-}}\ln(\frac{w_-(r+r_-)}{r-r_+})\right)\ ,

where the
r
appearing in the RHS should be thought as depending on
w_{\pm}
,
z_{p}
.

When using Poincaré coordinates to compute geodesics, we must keep in mind the region of Poincaré which describes Finkelstein space, namely
w_{+}>0
. Thus, when a geodesics is of the type
K_{+}K_{-}>0
, either it remains in the region
w_{+}>0
, and thus it is doubly anchored in Finkelstein space (see fig.
4.2
), or it reaches the
w_{+}=0
boundary. In this case, by (
D.22
), the geodesics escapes at
(v\rightarrow-\infty,r=r_{+},y\rightarrow-\infty)
. As such, it follows closely the apparent horizon towards the ”coordinate” horizon, both in time and space. Note that the divergence is such that
\frac{v}{y}\approx\frac{r_{+}}{r_{-}}
. In the context of a pure CFT, these geodesics do not contribute to the RT prescription, as they have only one anchor point. In ICFT, they might be ”stopped” by the membrane, and upon crossing to the other side, head back to the boundary, so it is plausible that they play a role in this case.

By (
4.54
), the outer horizon of the black hole is mapped to
w_{-}=0
. Thus, one might argue that it should be possible to enter the horizon of the black hole and head back to the boundary: a geodesic in Poincaré that dips in the
w_{-}<0
region and heads back to the boundary should generate such a horizon probing geodesic in Finkelstein coordinates. However, from (
4.9
), the curves
w_{\pm}(\lambda)
are obviously monotonic, barring such a possibility. Thus if a geodesic crosses the region
w_{-}<0
, it will remain in it and thus be unable to leave the black hole. Its precise fate depends on the geodesic, it could again escape at infinity before reaching the singularity if it reaches
w_{+}=0
, otherwise, it will reach
r=0
as
z_{p}\rightarrow 0
, as seen from (
D.22
). Again, these geodesics are irrelevant in the context of a pure CFT, but might be very important in the context of ICFT. If they intersect the membrane on their way to the singularity, that could give rise to geodesics of the type depicted in fig.
4.11
.

Consider now the case
K_{+}K_{-}<0
, see sec.
4.1.2
. Now, as explained in the main text, even in Poincaré we are in presence of two disconnected branches each with one anchor point on the boundary. Considering them as separate geodesics, we look at the mapping of one of the two branches to Finkelstein space. The discussion is very similar, namely, we must pay attention to the important surfaces
w_{\pm}=0
in Poincaré space. However, in this case as the affine parameter
\lambda
approaches
0
(we assume we begin with
\lambda_{0}<0
) we will have
w_{\pm}\rightarrow-{\rm Sign}(w_{\mp})\infty
. As such, we are assured to either enter the horizon at
w_{-}=0
, or escape at infinity at
w_{+}=0
. Indeed, as in this case the signs of
\dot{w}_{\pm}
are opposing, one of the two situations will happen.

We do not comment on the last case
K_{+}K_{-}=0
, as it is irrelevant to the numerical algorithms. Nothing special happens with respect to the two other cases, the same reasoning applies.
